Tab. (
2.2
) provides the SM and
B-L
charges of various scalars stemming from the irreps of
SO(10)
capable of contributing to the renormalisable Yukawa sector. Some of the scalars listed in the Tabs. (
2.1
and
2.2
) may have identical SM charges but differ in
B-L
charges; consequently, scalars with identical SM charges are denoted with a tilde (
\sim
) over them in Tab. (
2.1
).

In this section, we evaluate the couplings of scalar fields residing in the multiplets that participate in the Yukawa interactions at the renormalisable level. Various techniques have been devised to decompose
SO(10)
irreps based on the path (
i.e.
intermediate symmetry) to reach the SM gauge symmetry
[
Wilczek:1979hc
,
Mohapatra:1979nn
,
PhysRevD.53.3884
,
Aulakh:2002zr
,
Fukuyama:2004ps
]
. Furthermore, an explicit basis for various irreps of
SO(10)
has also been constructed in
[
Ozer:2005dwq
]
. One of the popular techniques to decompose invariants of
SO(10)
into those of
SU(5)
is via the oscillator expansion technique
[
Mohapatra:1979nn
,
Nath:2001uw
]
, which we discuss subsequently.

2.4.1
Oscillator Expansion of
SO(10)

Consider
N
mutually anticommuting creation
(b^{\dagger}_{i})
and annihilation
b_{i}
operators, where
1\leq i\leq N
operators.

\displaystyle{}\big\{b_{p},b^{\dagger}_{q}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{pq}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\big\{b_{p},b_{q}\big\}\,=\,\big\{b^{\dagger}_{p},b^{\dagger}_{q}\big\}\,=\,0

(2.4)

We define
2N
operators
\gamma_{p}
with
1\leq p\leq 2N
as follows:

\displaystyle{}\gamma_{2p-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-i\left(b_{p}-b^{\dagger}_{p}\right)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\gamma_{2p}\,=\,\left(b_{p}+b^{\dagger}_{p}\right)

(2.5)

It is straightforward to verify that the defined
\gamma
operators in Eq. (
2.5
) satisfy the Clifford algebra defined in Eq. (
A.1
). Without the need for the exact structure of the operators, one can yet define their action of the vacuum state
\big|0\rangle
.

\displaystyle{}\big|0\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\underbrace{\big|0,0,...,0\rangle}_{\rm{N}\,zeros},\hskip 28.45274ptb_{p}\,\big|0\rangle\,=\,0,\,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptb^{\dagger}_{p}\big|0\rangle\,=\,\underbrace{\big|0,0,.,1,.,0\rangle}_{\text{1 at p${}^{th}$ position}}

Further, defining
T_{pq}\equiv b^{\dagger}_{p}\,b_{q}
which satisfies the
U(N)
lie algebra. Therefore, any spinor representation
\Phi
can be written into antisymmetric tensors of
U(N)
,
i.e.

\phi
, and a bunch of creation and annihilation operators typically called oscillator basis, as shown below:

\displaystyle{}\big|\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{p=N}\frac{1}{p!}\,b^{\dagger}_{i_{1}}b^{\dagger}_{i_{2}}...b^{\dagger}_{i_{N}}\,\big|0\rangle\,\phi^{i_{1}i_{2}...i_{p}}.

(2.7)

The spinorial representation of
\mathbf{16}_{+}
(cf. Appendix (
A
)) in terms of creation and annihilation operators is as follows
[
Mohapatra:1979nn
]
:

\displaystyle{}\big|\mathbf{16}_{+}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big|0\rangle\mathbf{1}+\frac{1}{2}\,b^{\dagger}_{p}b^{\dagger}_{q}\,\big|0\rangle\,\mathbf{10}^{pq}+\frac{1}{4!}\,\varepsilon^{pqrst}\,b^{\dagger}_{p}b^{\dagger}_{q}b^{\dagger}_{r}b^{\dagger}_{s}\big|0\rangle\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{t},

(2.8)

\mathbf{1}
,
\mathbf{\overline{5}}
and
\mathbf{10}
are
SU(5)
irreps consisting of SM fermions and singlet fermions.

The decomposition of
\mathbf{\overline{5}}
into
SU(3)\,\times\,SU(2)\,\times U(1)
multiplets is shown below:

\displaystyle{}\mathbf{\overline{5}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\overline{3},1,\frac{1}{3}\right)\oplus\left(1,\overline{2},-\frac{1}{2}\right).

(2.9)

It is evident from the Eq. (
2.4.1
) that the decomposed multiplets have the same quantum numbers as those conjugated down quark and lepton doublet (cf. Tab. (
1.1
)).

Similarly,
\mathbf{10}
can be decomposed into SM invariant multiples:

\displaystyle{}\mathbf{10}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(3,2,\frac{1}{6}\right)\oplus\left(\overline{3},1,\frac{2}{3}\right)\oplus\left(1,1,1\right).

(2.10)

These decomposed multiplets shown in the Eq. (
2.4.1
) correspond to the SM charges of quark-doublet, conjugated up-quark and conjugated electron (cf. Tab.
1.1
).

The singlet under
SU(5)
can be associated with the missing ally of SM fermions,
i.e.
right-handed neutrino, as shown below;

\displaystyle{}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{C}.

(2.11)

Throughout this thesis, we denote generation indices by uppercase Latin alphabets
\big(1\leq A,B,C...\leq 3\big)
,
SU(5)
indices by lowercase Latin letters
\big(1\leq n,o,p,...,z\leq 5\big)
,
SU(3)
indices by lowercase Greek letters
\big(1\leq\alpha,\beta,\gamma...\leq 3\big)
, and lowercase Latin alphabets
\big(4\leq a,b,c,...,m\leq 5\big)
represent the
SU(2)
indices, unless specified explicitly. Moreover, bold letters denote fermion representations, while scalar and gauge representations are denoted with subscript
H
and
V
, respectively.

At this juncture, we also clarify our definitions of the two-indexed (alias the
SU(2)
)
Levi-Civita
symbols in alignment with the conventions
1
1

1

Interested readers can refer to the insightful footnote on page 12 of the review
[
Dreiner:2008tw
]
for other definitions of two-indexed
Levi-Civita
tensors
for Weyl spinors mentioned in
[
Dreiner:2008tw
]
. The two rank
SU(2)\;Levi-Civita
tensor used is defined as
\varepsilon_{45}=-\varepsilon_{54}=-\varepsilon^{45}=\varepsilon^{54}=1
, while the three rank
SU(3)\;Levi-Civita
tensor is defined as
\varepsilon_{123}=\varepsilon^{123}=1
, and similarly for all other cyclic permutations. The transformation rule for
SU(2)
tensor is consistent with the aforementioned two indexed Levi-Civita tensor definitions, which are as follows:

\displaystyle{}\psi_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \varepsilon_{ab}\,\psi^{b},\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\psi^{a}\,=\,\varepsilon^{ab}\,\psi_{b}.

(2.12)

Further,
\varepsilon_{ab}\,\varepsilon^{cd}\,=\,-\delta_{a}^{c}\,\delta_{b}^{d}\,+\,\delta_{a}^{d}\,\delta_{b}^{c}
, and
\varepsilon_{\alpha\theta\phi}\,\varepsilon^{\beta\theta\phi}\,=\,2\,\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}
. With the notations established for
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
indices, along with other essential conventions, we can now define the explicit content of the
\mathbf{\overline{5}}
and
\mathbf{10}
irreps in terms of SM fermions. In matrix notation, the contents of
\mathbf{\overline{5}}
dimensional irrep are shown below;

\displaystyle{}\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d^{C}_{\alpha},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{a}=\varepsilon_{ab}\,l^{b}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\overline{5}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}d^{C}_{\alpha}\\
\varepsilon_{ab}\,l^{b}\end{pmatrix}\;=\;\hskip 14.22636pt\begin{pmatrix}d^{C}_{1}\\
d^{C}_{2}\\
d^{C}_{3}\\
e\\
-\nu\end{pmatrix},\;

(2.13)

where
d_{1,2,3}
represents the colour degrees of freedom of the down quark. Further,
{\cal C}
is the charge conjugation matrix in the Lorentz space as defined in Eq. (
1.3
). Similarly, the particle content of
\mathbf{10}
-dimensional irrep is shown below:

\displaystyle{}\mathbf{10}^{a\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q^{a\,\alpha},\hskip 14.22636pt\;\mathbf{10}^{\alpha\,\beta}\,=\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{C}_{\gamma},\;\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbf{10}^{ab}\,=\,\varepsilon^{ab}\,e^{C}.

\displaystyle\mathbf{10}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&u^{C}_{3}&-u^{C}_{2}&u^{1}&d^{1}\\
-u^{C}_{3}&0&u^{C}_{1}&u^{2}&d^{2}\\
u^{C}_{2}&-u^{C}_{1}&0&u^{3}&d^{3}\\
-u^{1}&-u^{2}&-u^{3}&0&-e^{C}\\
-d^{1}&-d^{2}&-d^{3}&e^{C}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(2.14)

2.4.2
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
couplings

We commence the decomposition of the coupling of
10_{\text{H}}
with the
\mathbf{16}
-plet using the technique outlined in section (
2.4.1
).

\displaystyle{}\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\mathbf{16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\left<\mathbf{16}^{*}_{+\,A}\big|\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,{\cal B}\,\Gamma_{\mu}\,\phi_{\mu}\,\big|\mathbf{16}_{+\,B}\right>

(2.15)

{\mathcal{B}}
is the charge conjugation operator in
SO(10)
space and is defined in terms of creation and annihilation operators as follows
[
Mohapatra:1979nn
,
Nath:2001uw
]
;

\displaystyle{}{\cal B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\,\prod_{p=1}^{5}\,\left(b_{p}-b_{p}^{\dagger}\right).

(2.16)

The action of the charge conjugation operator
({\cal B})
, defined in the Eq. (
2.16
), on the
\mathbf{16}
-spinor, defined in Eq. (
2.8
), can be computed and is given as follows
[
Mohapatra:1979nn
,
Nath:2001uw
,
Cardoso:2015gfa
]
;

\displaystyle{}\langle\mathbf{16}^{*}_{+}\big|\,{\cal B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p}\langle 0\big|b_{p}-i\,\frac{1}{12}\,\mathbf{10}^{pq}\,\varepsilon_{pqrst}\,\langle 0\big|b_{r}b_{s}\,b_{t}-i\,\mathbf{1}\langle 0\big|b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}b_{4}b_{5}.

Similarly,
10_{H}
can also be defined in terms of creation and annihilation operators as follows
[
Mohapatra:1979nn
,
Nath:2001uw
]
;

\displaystyle{}\Gamma_{\mu}\,10_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b^{\dagger}_{p}\,\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{p}+b_{p}\,\tilde{\overline{5}}_{p\,{\mathrm{H}}},

(2.18)

\phi_{\mu}
being a real-scalar, its kinetic term is as follows:

\displaystyle{}{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm{KE}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}10_{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}\,10_{\mu}\right)

(2.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}5_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}5_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right)+\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right),

where
\dot{\mu}\,\epsilon\,\{0,1,2,3\}
and are the Lorentz indices, as defined in Chapter (
1
). To have canonically normalised kinetic energy term we set,
\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\equiv\sqrt{2}\,5_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\tilde{\overline{5}}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\equiv\sqrt{2}\,\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. The fundamental aspect of canonical normalisation of the kinetic term lies in its ability to establish the energy-momentum relation in a conventional form, facilitating standard interpretations. Using Eqs. (
2.8
,
2.4.2
, and
2.18
), it is straightforward to derive the expression written in Eq. (
2.15
) and it is shown below, where we have omitted
\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,
for brevity;

\displaystyle{}\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\left<\mathbf{16}^{*}_{+\,A}\big|B\,\Gamma_{\mu}\,10_{\mu}\,\big|\mathbf{16}_{+\,B}\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\big<\mathbf{16}^{*}_{A}\big|B\,\left(b^{\dagger}_{t}\,\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{t}+b_{t}\,\tilde{\overline{5}}_{t\,{\mathrm{H}}}\right)

(2.20)

\displaystyle\times\,\bigg(\big|0\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\,b^{\dagger}_{p}b^{\dagger}_{q}\,\big|0\rangle\,\mathbf{10}^{pq}_{B}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4!}\,\varepsilon^{pqrst}\,b^{\dagger}_{p}b^{\dagger}_{q}b^{\dagger}_{r}b^{\dagger}_{s}b^{\dagger}_{t}\big|0\rangle\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{B}\bigg).

It is worth mentioning that the number of creation and annihilation operators must be equal for a non-zero contribution of the matrix element. The contribution involving
\mathbf{1}
-plet is as follows:

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\ i\,\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{m\,A}\,\langle 0\big|\,b_{m}\,b^{\dagger}_{t}\big|0\rangle\,\mathbf{1}_{B}\,\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{t}

(2.21)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{i}{24}\,H_{AB}\,\mathbf{1}_{A}\langle 0\big|b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}b_{4}b_{5}\,b^{\dagger}_{t}b^{\dagger}_{p}b^{\dagger}_{q}b^{\dagger}_{r}b^{\dagger}_{s}\big|0\rangle\varepsilon^{pqrsu}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{u\,B}\,\tilde{5}^{t}_{{\mathrm{H}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{m\,A}\,\delta^{m}_{t}\,\mathbf{1}_{B}\,\tilde{5}^{t}-H_{AB}\,\frac{i}{24}\,\varepsilon_{12345}\,\varepsilon^{tpqrs}\,\varepsilon^{pqrsu}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{u\,B}\,5^{t}_{{\mathrm{H}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left(\tilde{H}_{AB}+\tilde{H}_{BA}\right)\,\mathbf{1}_{A}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p\,B}\,\tilde{5}^{p}_{{\mathrm{H}}},

where, we set
\frac{1}{2}\left(\tilde{H}_{AB}+\tilde{H}_{BA}\right)\equiv H_{AB}
, making
H
symmetric under generation labels and using canonically normalised definitions of
\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,=\,\sqrt{2}\,5_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\tilde{\overline{5}}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,=\,\sqrt{2}\,\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. Eq. (
2.21
) reduces to the following:

\displaystyle{}-i\,2\sqrt{2}\,H_{AB}\,\mathbf{1}_{A}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p\,B}\,5^{p}_{{\mathrm{H}}}.

(2.22)

Similarly, for the other term, we have the following.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\frac{i}{24}\,\varepsilon_{stpqr}\mathbf{10}^{st}_{A}\langle 0\big|b_{p}b_{q}b_{r}b^{\dagger}_{u}b^{\dagger}_{v}b^{\dagger}_{w}\big|0\rangle\tilde{5}^{u}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\mathbf{10}^{vw}_{B}

(2.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\frac{i}{24}\,\varepsilon_{stpqr}\,\mathbf{10}^{st}_{A}\,\frac{1}{2!}\,\varepsilon^{xypqr}\,\varepsilon_{xyuvw}\,\tilde{5}^{t}_{H}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{vw}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\frac{i}{24}\,\mathbf{10}^{st}_{A}\,\frac{3!}{2!}\,\left(-\delta^{x}_{s}\delta^{y}_{t}+\delta^{x}_{t}\delta^{y}_{s}\right)\,\tilde{5}^{t}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{vw}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{H}_{AB}\,\frac{i}{4}\,\varepsilon_{stvwu}\,\mathbf{10}_{A}^{st}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{vw}\,\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\;=\;\frac{i}{8}\left(\frac{\tilde{H}_{AB}+\tilde{H}_{BA}}{2}\right)\,2\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{stvwu}\,\mathbf{10}_{A}^{st}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{vw}\,5_{{\mathrm{H}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{AB}\,\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{8}\,\varepsilon_{stvwu}\,\mathbf{10}_{A}^{st}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{vw}\,5_{{\mathrm{H}}}

Similarly, the derivation of the remaining term is straightforward, and the final result of the interactions of
{\bf 16}
-plet fermions with
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
becomes the following
[
Nath:2001uw
]
:

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{AB}\,{\bf 16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,{\bf 16}_{B}\,{\bf 10}_{H}\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

(2.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\,2\sqrt{2}\,H_{AB}\,\left({\mathbf{10}^{pq}_{A}}^{T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{pB}\,\overline{5}_{q\,H}\,+\,\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{stuvw}\,\mathbf{10}^{st\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{10}^{uv}_{B}\,5^{m}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right.

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{A}^{T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{iB}\,5^{i}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\bigg)\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

The expression given above in Eq. (
2.24
) is invariant under
SU(5)
. Also, all the scalar
SU(5)
irreps appearing in the same expression are defined with canonically normalised kinetic terms.

Further, using the decomposition of
\mathbf{1}
,
\mathbf{\overline{5}}
and
\mathbf{10}
dimensional irreps in terms of SM fermions using Eqs. (
2.11
,
2.4.1
, and
2.4.1
), we can decompose the
SU(5)
invariant expression given in Eq. (
2.24
) to SM invariant. For this, the scalars identified in
5_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
can be decomposed into the following,

\displaystyle 5_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{\alpha}\equiv T^{\alpha}\,,~~5_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{a}\equiv D^{a}\,,~~\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}\,\alpha}\equiv\overline{T}_{\alpha}\,,\text{and}~~\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}\,a}\equiv\overline{D}_{a}\,,

(2.25)

manner ensuring their kinetic terms are canonically normalised. Substituting Eqs. (
2.11
,
2.4.1
,
2.4.1
, and
2.25
) into Eq. (
2.24
) yields the coupling of SM fermions with the scalars residing in
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, as shown below:

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/5_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle i2\sqrt{2}\,H_{AB}\,\Bigg[-\bigg(u^{C\,T}_{\gamma A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,e^{C}_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\,\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{b\,\beta}_{B}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\gamma\,B}\,\bigg)T^{\gamma}\,

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left.\bigg(\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\alpha\,B}\,-\varepsilon_{ab}\,\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{a}_{B}\,\bigg)\,D^{b}\right]\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle i2\sqrt{2}\,H_{AB}\,\Bigg[\bigg(\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{C\,T}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\beta B}+\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\,\gamma\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{b}_{B}\bigg)\overline{T}_{\gamma}

(2.26)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle q^{a\,\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{B}\,\overline{D}_{a}\Bigg]\;+\;\rm{H.c.}.

The following points regarding the different vertices appearing in Eq. (
2.4.2
) are worth noting.

(i)

In addition to the known couplings of SM-Higgs with SM fermions, the decomposition of
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
yields interactions in which
T
and
\overline{T}
have diquark and leptoquark vertices.

(ii)

The inclusion of only
10_{\text{H}}
dimensional irrep in the Yukawa sector results in
Y_{u}=Y_{d}=Y_{e}^{T}=Y_{\nu^{C}}=H
at the GUT scale, depicting a generational mass degeneracy in all the sector.

(iii)

T^{\gamma}
couples to
\nu^{C}\,d^{C}
and
\nu^{C}\,l
. We will utilise this specific coupling in Chapter (
6
) to address the removal of mass degeneracy in the down-quark and charged lepton sector within the context of
SU(5)
.

Employing a similar procedure, one can also compute the decomposition of
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
which is done in subsequent chapters. Now, we will outline the decomposition of gauge interactions in renormalisable
SO(10)
.

2.5
Gauge Interactions in Renormalisable
SO(10)

The coupling of
45
-dimensional irrep consisting of gauge bosons with
\mathbf{16}
can be parameterised as follows:

\displaystyle{}L_{AB}\,\mathbf{16}_{A}^{\dagger}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\rho}}\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\,45_{{\mathrm{V}}\dot{\rho}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle L_{AB}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\mathbf{16}_{-A}^{*}\big|\,{\cal B}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\rho}}\,\Phi_{\mu\nu}\,\big|\mathbf{16}_{+B}\rangle\,45_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\mu\nu\,\dot{\rho}},

(2.27)

where
L_{AB}
is a matrix in generation space and for our case
L_{AB}\,=\,\delta_{AB}
.

The expansion of
\Phi_{\mu\nu}\,45_{\mu\nu}
is terms of creation and annihilation operators is as follows
[
Nath:2001yj
]
:

\displaystyle{}\Phi_{\mu\nu}\,45_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{i}\,\left(b_{p}\,b_{q}\,\tilde{10}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{V}}\,pq}+b^{\dagger}_{p}\,b^{\dagger}_{q}\,\tilde{10}_{{\mathrm{V}}}^{pq}+2\,b^{\dagger}_{p}\,b_{q}\,\tilde{24}^{p}_{{\mathrm{V}}\,q}+\big(\frac{2}{5}\,b^{\dagger}_{p}\,b_{q}\delta^{p}_{q}-1\big)\tilde{1}_{{\mathrm{V}}}\right).

Following a similar procedure outlined in Eq. (
2.19
), canonical normalisation of
SU(5)
irreps residing within
45
yields the following field redefinition
[
Nath:2001yj
]
.

\displaystyle{}\tilde{24}_{{\mathrm{V}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\,24_{{\mathrm{V}}},~~~~\tilde{1}_{{\mathrm{V}}}\,=\,\sqrt{10}\,1_{{\mathrm{V}}},

\displaystyle\tilde{10}_{{\mathrm{V}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\,10_{{\mathrm{V}}},~~~~\tilde{10}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{V}}}\,=\,\sqrt{2}\,10_{{\mathrm{V}}}.

(2.29)

The action of charge conjugation operator,
{\cal B}
, on
\mathbf{16}^{\dagger}
can be computed as follows
[
Cardoso:2015gfa
]
:

\displaystyle{}\langle\mathbf{16}_{-A}^{*}\big|\,{\cal B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\mathbf{1}^{\dagger}_{A}\langle 0\big|+\frac{i}{2}\,\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}_{no\,A}\langle 0\big|b_{n}b_{o}+\frac{i}{24}\,\varepsilon_{nopqr}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}^{\dagger\,n}_{A}\,\langle 0\big|b_{o}b_{p}b_{q}b_{r}.

(2.30)

Using Eqs. (
2.8
,
2.5
and
2.30
), one can evaluate the expression mentioned in Eq. (
2.27
). We evaluate the same expression by explicitly computing the coupling of
24
-dimensional gauge irrep with fermion-irreps of
SU(5)
, as done below;

\displaystyle L_{AB}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\mathbf{16}_{-A}^{*}\big|\,{\cal B}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\rho}}\,\Phi_{\mu\nu}\,\big|\mathbf{16}_{+B}\rangle\,45_{\mu\nu\,\dot{\rho}}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle L_{AB}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\langle\mathbf{16}_{-A}^{*}\big|\,{\cal B}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\rho}}\,\left(b^{\dagger}_{s}b_{t}\,\tilde{24}^{s}_{t}\right)\,b_{p}^{\dagger}b_{q}^{\dagger}\big|0\rangle\,\tilde{10}^{pq}_{\dot{\rho}}.

It is easy to see that only the second term of Eq. (
2.30
) contributes to the non-zero matrix element. Using,

\displaystyle{}\langle 0\big|b_{i}b_{j}b^{\dagger}_{k}b_{l}b^{\dagger}_{m}b^{\dagger}_{n}\,\big|0\rangle\,=\,\delta_{lm}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{in}\,-\,\delta_{jn}\delta_{ik}\right)\,-\,\delta_{ln}\left(\delta_{jk}\delta_{im}\,-\,\delta_{jm}\delta_{ik}\right),

(2.32)

the last line given in Eq. (
2.31
) reduces to following:

\displaystyle{}\frac{4\,L_{AB}}{4}\,10^{\dagger}_{pq\,A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,10^{qr}_{B}\,\tilde{24}^{p}_{r\,\dot{\mu}}.

(2.33)

Using the canonically normalised
\tilde{24}
given in Eq. (
2.5
), coupling of
\tilde{24}
modifies as follows:

\displaystyle{}L_{AB}\,\sqrt{2}\,10^{\dagger}_{pq}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,10^{qr}_{B}\,\tilde{24}^{p}_{r\,\dot{\mu}}.

(2.34)

Following the same procedure, the final expression of
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-45_{{\mathrm{V}}}
in terms of canonically normalised
SU(5)
tensors is shown as follows
[
Nath:2001yj
]
:

\displaystyle{}{\cal L}^{(45)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L_{AB}\,\Bigg[\sqrt{5}\left(-\frac{3}{5}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{A}^{\dagger\,p}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{B\,p}+\frac{1}{10}\,\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}_{A\,pq}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{10}^{pq}+\mathbf{1}^{\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{1}_{B}\right)1_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\dot{\mu}}

(2.35)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}\left(\mathbf{1}^{\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{pq}+\frac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon^{rstpq}\,\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}_{A\,rs}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{B\,t}\right)\,10^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\dot{\mu}pq}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}_{A\,pq}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{1}_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{rstpq}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{A}^{\dagger\,r}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{st}\right)\,10_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\dot{\mu}}^{pq}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\left(\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}_{A\,pr}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{rq}+\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{A}^{\dagger\,q}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{Bp}\right)\,24_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\dot{\mu}q}^{p}\Bigg].

The above expression is invariant under
SU(5)
. To decompose the
SU(5)
invariant expression given in Eq. (
2.35
) into SM invariant, we further decompose
10
-dimensional irrep into canonically normalised tensors, as shown below:

\displaystyle{}10^{ab}_{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{t}^{ab},\;\;\;10^{a\alpha}_{V}\,=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,Y^{a\alpha},\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;10^{\alpha\beta}_{V}\,=\,\tilde{\Theta}^{\alpha\beta}.

(2.36)

Further, the decomposition of the
24
-dimensional irrep into its canonically normalised fields is shown below;

\displaystyle{}24^{\alpha}_{{\mathrm{V}}\,a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}_{a},\hskip 51.21504pt~~~~~~~~~~24^{a}_{{\mathrm{V}}\,\alpha}\,=\,\overline{X}^{a}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle 24^{\alpha}_{{\mathrm{V}}\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G^{\alpha}_{\beta}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{15}}\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,\hat{\sigma},\hskip 28.45274pt24^{a}_{{\mathrm{V}}\,b}=W^{a}_{b}-\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{15}}\,\delta^{a}_{b}\,\hat{\sigma}.

(2.37)

The SM charges of the above gauge fields appearing in Eqs. (
2.36
and
2.5
) can be inferred from Tab. (
2.1
). The tracelessness of
24_{{\mathrm{H}}}
is the sole reason for the doublet-triplet splitting problem in
SU(5)
-GUTs
[
Masiero:1982fe
,
Grinstein:1982um
,
Babu:2006nf
]
. Tracelessness indicates that various degrees of freedom of different scalars are interconnected and cannot be chosen arbitrarily.

Using Eqs. (
2.11
,
2.4.1
,
2.4.1
,
2.36
, and
2.5
), the expression given in Eq. (
2.35
) can be decomposed into SM invariant expression consisting of SM fermions. Below, we show the vertices of gauge bosons,
i.e.

X
and
Y
, having diquark and leptoquark couplings that can induce proton decay.

\displaystyle{}{\cal L}^{X\&Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,L_{AB}\,\Bigg[\sqrt{2}\,\Big(\varepsilon_{ab}\big(d^{C}_{\alpha\,A}\big)^{\dagger}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,l^{b}_{B}\,-\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\big(q^{a\,\beta}_{A}\big)^{\dagger}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,u^{C}_{\gamma\,B}

(2.38)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,\big(e^{C}_{A}\big)^{\dagger}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,q^{b\alpha}_{B}\Big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\Big(\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,d^{C\,\dagger}_{\beta\,A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,q^{b\gamma}_{B}-\big(l^{a}\big)^{\dagger}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,u^{C}_{\alpha\,B}\Big)\Bigg]\overline{X}^{a}_{\dot{\mu}\alpha}\;+\;\rm{H.c.}.

2.6
Yukawa Interactions in Non-Renormalisable
SO(10)

In section (
2.4
), we have considered irreps, contributing to the Yukawa Lagrangian at the renormalisable level. These tensors,
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, have large degree of freedoms and incorporating large tensor representations (LTR) like
120_{\rm{H}},\overline{126}_{\rm{H}},
and
210_{\rm{H}}
in the scalar sector affects low-energy phenomenology. However, these LTRs affect the
\beta
-functions, causing low-energy gauge couplings to rise with energy and potentially hitting the Landau pole before the Planck Scale, a phenomenon known as asymptotic slavery in
SO(10)
gauge theories
[
Maiani:1977cg
,
Vaughn:1978st
,
Rubakov:1999mu
,
Aulakh:2002ww
,
Aulakh:2002ph
,
Aulakh:2003kg
,
Bajc:2016efj
]
. To avoid this, smaller tensor representations like
10_{\rm{H}},16_{\rm{H}},45_{\rm{H}},
and
54_{\rm{H}}
and non-renormalisable interactions can be used
[
Babu:1994dc
,
Dvali:1996wh
,
Barr:1997hq
,
Babu:1998wi
,
Chang:2004pb
,
Sayre:2006wf
,
Preda:2022izo
]
. These interactions could stem from another theory valid above a certain cutoff scale
(\Lambda)
. Likewise, the SM uses renormalisable interactions for charged fermion masses and non-renormalisable ones for neutral fermions.

16_{H}
is the smallest representation contributing to both charged fermion and neutrino masses, albeit at the non-renormalisable level. As a result, it is the most phenomenologically attractive choice to study the non-renormalisable interactions in
SO(10)
. The various scalars contained in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, along with their SM charge and
B-L
quantum number, are illustrated in Tab. (
2.3
). As evident from Table (
2.3
), some of the scalars may possess identical SM charges to those shown in Table (
2.2
). Consequently, the scalars present in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
are represented with a
\hat{}
symbol over them. All the scalars residing in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
have a non-zero
B-L
charge. The canonically normalised decomposition of scalars residing in the
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
-dimensional irrep is as follows
[
Patel:2022wya
]
:

\displaystyle{}1_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma},\hskip 28.45274pt5^{\dagger}_{a\,\,H}\;=\;\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{a},\hskip 28.45274pt5^{\dagger}_{\alpha\,\,H}\;=\;\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle 10^{ab}_{H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{t}^{ab},\,\hskip 28.45274pt10^{a\,\alpha}\;=\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\hat{\Delta}^{a\alpha},\hskip 28.45274pt10^{\alpha\beta}\;=\;\hat{\Theta}^{\alpha\beta}.

(2.39)

SM Charges

Notation

B-L
Charges

\left(1,1,0\right)

\hat{\sigma}

1

\left(\overline{3},1,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}\right)

\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}

\nicefrac{{-1}}{{3}}

\left(1,2,\nicefrac{{-1}}{{2}}\right)

\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{a}

-1

\left(3,2,\nicefrac{{1}}{{6}}\right)

\hat{\Delta}^{a\alpha}

\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}

\left(\overline{3},1,\nicefrac{{-2}}{{3}}\right)

\hat{\Theta}^{\alpha\beta}

\nicefrac{{-1}}{{3}}

\left(1,1,-1\right)

\hat{t}^{ab}

1

Table 2.3:
Classification of scalars present in
16_{H}
alongwith their SM and
B-L
charges

An
SO(10)
Lagrangian featuring the interactions of an
\mathbf{16}
-plet and a scalar
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
dimensional irrep can be parameterised to first order in
\Lambda
,
\Lambda
being the typical cutoff scale, as follows;

\displaystyle{}-{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Lambda}y_{AB}\,\big(\mathbf{16}_{A}\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\;16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\big)\,+\,\frac{1}{\Lambda}\bar{y}_{AB}\,\big(\mathbf{16}_{A}\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\;16^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,16^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\big)\,

(2.40)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\rm{H.c.}.

where,
y,\,\overline{y}
are the Yukawa couplings and depicts the strength of non-renormalisable interactions. To decompose down the Lagrangian provided in Eq. (
2.40
), one needs to account for all the Yukawa couplings effectively generated by integrating all possible irreps (scalar or fermions) consistent with the assumed gauge symmetry yielding the interaction of
\mathbf{16}-16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. The transformation properties of the
16
-dimensional irrep under
SO(10)
, as indicated in Eq. (
(iii)
), suggest that a term like the one in Eq. (
2.40
) allows only a limited number of intermediate
SO(10)
irreps are required to generate the effective coupling. One of the various ways to generate the coupling is specified in Eq. (
2.40
). We represent the coupling as
(...)_{D_{1}}(...)_{D_{2}}
, where the representations inside the first (second) bracket are contracted to form
D_{1}\,(D_{2})
, ensuring that
D_{1}\times D_{2}\supset\mathds{1}
, where
\mathds{1}
transforms trivially under
SO(10)
. Additionally,
D_{1}\,(D_{2})
can be either a scalar or a fermion representation;

\displaystyle\bullet

\displaystyle\frac{h_{AB}}{\Lambda}\big(\mathbf{16}_{A}\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\big)_{10_{H}}\,\big(16_{H}\,16_{H}\big)_{10_{H}};\;\frac{\bar{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\big(\mathbf{16}_{A}\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\big)_{10_{H}}\,\big(16^{\dagger}_{H}\,16^{\dagger}_{H}\big)_{10^{\dagger}_{H}};

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\big(\mathbf{16}_{A}\,16_{H}\big)_{\mathbf{10}}\,\big(\mathbf{16}_{B}\,16_{H}\big)_{\mathbf{10}};\text{and}\;\frac{\hat{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big(\mathbf{16}_{A}\,16_{H}\big)_{\mathbf{10}}\,\Big(\mathbf{16}_{B}\,16_{H}\Big)^{*}_{\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}};

where,
h,\,\bar{h},\tilde{h},\text{and}\,\hat{h}
are various Yukawa couplings and
\Lambda
corresponds to the energy scale where the intermediate
SO(10)
irrep has been integrated out or any other cut-off scale.

We illustrate a specific example that leads to the terms provided in Eq. (
2.40
) by integrating out the
10
-dimensional irrep. The relevant Lagrangian for the same is shown below, where we assume that only integration of
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
can yield the required non-renormalisable vertex;

\displaystyle{}-\cal{L}^{\mathrm{h}}_{\mathrm{NR}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\bigg(H\,\mathbf{16}^{T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{16}\,{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}+\eta\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\bigg)+M^{2}_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\,10^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}.

The
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
coupling have been already computed in Eq. (
2.24
). To obtain the
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}-16_{{\mathrm{H}}}-10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
coupling, we assume its form is analogous to the
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
coupling, where the
SU(5)
irreps of fermions of
\mathbf{16}
are replaced with the scalars residing in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
with the same
SU(5)
charge. The decomposition of the scalar part of Lagrangian given in Eq. (
2.6
) is as follows:

\displaystyle{}-{\cal{L}}^{h}_{\mathrm{NR}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\eta\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}+M^{2}_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\,10^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,

(2.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i2\sqrt{2}\,\eta\,\left(10_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{pq}\,5^{\dagger}_{p\,{\mathrm{H}}}\,\tilde{5}^{\dagger}_{q\,{\mathrm{H}}}\,+\,\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{pqrst}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{pq}\,5_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{rs}\,\tilde{5}^{t}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,-\,1_{\mathrm{H}}\,5^{\dagger}_{p}\,\tilde{5}^{p}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle M^{2}_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\,\tilde{5}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\,.

To integrate the
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
irrep, we utilise the equations of motion to solve for the various
SU(5)
components of
10_{H}
shown in Eq. (
2.43
), and then substitute these results into Eq. (
2.24
)
[
Nath:2001yj
]
. We must note that we integrate out those
SU(5)
irreps which have the same
U(1)_{\mathrm{X}}
charge. For example, the
U(1)_{\mathrm{X}}
charge of
\tilde{5}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\subset 16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
is
3
, whereas the
U(1)_{\mathrm{X}}
charge of
5^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\subset 10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
is
-2

[
Slansky:1981yr
,
Feger:2019tvk
]
. Therefore, identical
SU(5)
representations belonging to different
SO(10)
representations can have different
U(1)_{\mathrm{X}}
charges. Consequently, we denote the
SU(5)
scalars (and fermions) intended for integration by placing a tilde (
\sim
) over them.
Further, we also identify
\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
as
5^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
in previous expressions of effective couplings in section (
2.4
).

Integrating out
\tilde{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
from Eq. (
2.43
) and substituting the result into
{\cal L}^{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}/5_{{\mathrm{H}}}}
, and similarly, integrating out
\tilde{5}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
from Eq. (
2.43
) and substituting it into
{\cal L}^{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}/\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}
, where
{\cal L}^{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}/5_{{\mathrm{H}}}}
and
{\cal L}^{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}/\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}
are given in Eq. (
2.24
), allows us to derive the effective non-renormalisable interactions as shown below;

\displaystyle{}-\mathcal{L}^{h}_{\rm{NR}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\frac{h_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big(\mathbf{16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\Big)_{10}\,\Big(16_{H}\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\Big)_{10}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-8\,h_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big[\big(\mathbf{10}^{pq\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p\,B}\big)\,\big(-1_{H}\,5^{\dagger}_{q\,H}+\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{qrstu}\,10^{rs}_{H}\,10^{tu}_{H}\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\big(-\mathbf{1}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p\,B}\frac{1}{8}\,\varepsilon_{qrstp}\,\mathbf{10}^{qr\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\mathbf{10}^{st}_{B}\big)\,\big(10^{pw}_{H}\,5^{\dagger}_{w\,H}\big)\Big]\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

where, we set
\frac{\eta\,H_{AB}}{M^{2}_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}}\,\,\equiv\,\frac{h_{AB}}{\Lambda}
. The expression given in Eq. (
2.6
) is invariant under
SU(5)
and can be further decomposed using the dictionary provided in Eqs. (
2.11
), (
2.4.1
), (
2.4.1
), and (
2.6
) into SM invariant vertices. The resulting expressions is:

\displaystyle{}-\mathcal{L}^{h}_{\rm{NR}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\frac{h_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big(\mathbf{16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\Big)_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\,\Big(16_{H}\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\Big)_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

(2.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-8\,h_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big[\big(\mathbf{10}^{pq\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p\,B}\big)\,\big(-1_{H}\,5^{\dagger}_{q\,H}+\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{qrstu}\,10^{rs}_{H}\,10^{tu}_{H}\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\big(-\mathbf{1}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{p\,B}+\frac{1}{8}\,\varepsilon_{qrstp}\,\mathbf{10}^{qr\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\mathbf{10}^{st}_{B}\big)\,\big(10^{pw}_{H}\,5^{\dagger}_{w\,H}\big)\Big]\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-h_{AB}}{\Lambda}\,\bigg[-\left(4\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\alpha\,B}\right)\left(\hat{\Delta}^{b\theta}\,\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\theta}+\sqrt{2}\,\hat{t}^{bd}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{d}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\bigg(-4\varepsilon_{ab}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{b\beta}_{B}-8u^{C\,T}_{\gamma\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,e^{C}_{B}\bigg)\,\bigg(-\frac{\hat{\Delta}^{d\gamma}}{\sqrt{2}}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{d}+\hat{\Theta}^{\gamma\lambda}\,\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\lambda}\bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{c}_{B}+e^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{a}_{B}\right)\,\left(\sqrt{2}\varepsilon_{ab}\,\varepsilon_{\beta\gamma\lambda}\,\hat{\Delta}^{b\beta}\,\hat{\Theta}^{\gamma\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha\theta\rho}\,u^{C\,T}_{\theta\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\rho\,B}+\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{t\,\alpha}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{b}\right)\,2\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\varepsilon_{mn}\left(\hat{\Theta}^{\beta\gamma}\hat{t}^{mn}-\hat{\Delta}^{m\beta}\,\hat{\Delta}^{n\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 8\left(\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\alpha}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{b}_{B}+\varepsilon^{\beta\gamma\alpha}\,u^{C\,T}_{\beta\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\gamma\,B}\right)\,\hat{\sigma}\,\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(e^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{a}_{B}+q^{a\alpha\,T}_{B}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\alpha}\right)\,\hat{\sigma}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{A}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 8\bigg(\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\alpha}\bigg)\,\bigg(-\frac{\hat{\Delta}^{a\alpha}}{\sqrt{2}}+\hat{\Theta}^{\alpha\beta}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\beta}\bigg)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 8\bigg(\varepsilon_{ab}\,\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{a}_{B}\bigg)\,\bigg(\frac{\hat{\Delta}^{a\alpha}}{\sqrt{2}}+\hat{t}^{bc}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{c}\bigg)\bigg]\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

The above expression shows the mass dimension five coupling of SM fermions with the scalars residing in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. A simple-looking expression, written in Eq. (
2.40
) when decomposed, gives a plethora of interactions.

So far, we have integrated out
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
-irrep to generate the effective coupling mentioned in Eq. (

). We further provide other possibilities for generating the same effective coupling. Consider the following Lagrangian;

\displaystyle{}-{\cal{L}}^{\overline{h}}_{\mathrm{NR}}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle\bigg(H\,\mathbf{16}^{T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{16}\,{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}+\left(\eta\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,16_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right)^{\dagger}\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\bigg)+M^{2}_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\,10^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,10_{{\mathrm{H}}}.

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\rm{H.c.}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big(\mathbf{16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,16_{H}\big)_{\mathbf{10}}\,\Big(\mathbf{16}^{T}_{B}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,16_{H}\Big)^{*}_{\mathbf{10}^{\dagger}}\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

\displaystyle\Big[\frac{-8\,\tilde{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\big(\mathbf{10}^{pq\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,5^{\dagger}_{p\,H}\big)\,\big(-\mathbf{1}_{B}\,5^{\dagger}_{q\,H}+\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{qrstu}\,\mathbf{10}_{B}^{rs}\,10^{tu}_{H}\big)\,+A\leftrightarrow B\Big]\,

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Big[\big(\mathbf{10}^{pq\,T}_{A}\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,5_{r\,H}^{\dagger}\big)\,\big(\mathbf{10}^{pq}_{B}\,\,5_{r\,H}^{\dagger}\big)^{*}+\big(\mathbf{\overline{5}}^{T}_{p\,A}\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,10^{qr}_{H}\big)\,\big(\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{pB}\,10^{qr}_{H}\big)^{*}

\displaystyle\times\,\big(-\mathbf{1}_{B}\,5^{\dagger}_{t\,H}+\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{xywzt}\,\mathbf{10}^{xy}_{B}10^{wz}_{H}\big)^{*}\Big]\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{8\,\tilde{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\Bigg[\varepsilon^{\rho\beta\alpha}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\theta\gamma}\left[\left(2u^{C\,T}_{\rho\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,e^{C}_{B}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\beta}\,\hat{\Theta}^{\theta\gamma}\right)\right.

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left.\left(\varepsilon_{mn}\,u^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{m\,\Theta}_{B}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\beta}\,\frac{\hat{\Delta}^{n\gamma}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{mn}\,\left[\left(q^{m\beta\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\alpha\,B}\right)\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\beta}\,\frac{\hat{\Delta}^{n\alpha}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\varepsilon_{\alpha\gamma\theta}}{2}\left(q^{n\beta\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{m\alpha}_{B}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\beta}\hat{\Theta}^{\gamma\theta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,e^{C}_{B}\right)\,\left(\varepsilon^{mp}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{m}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{p}\right)+\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\left(\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\beta\,B}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\gamma}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle A\leftrightarrow B

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{8\hat{h}_{AB}}{\Lambda}\bigg[\left(e^{C\,T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(e^{C}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{H}^{a}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{a}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\theta\phi}\,u^{C\,T}_{\beta\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(u^{C}_{\theta}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\gamma}\,\hat{T}^{\phi}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{a\beta}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\alpha}\,\hat{T}^{\beta}\right)+\left(q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{b\alpha}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{H}^{a}\,\hat{H}^{b}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon^{\theta\phi\lambda}\,\left(q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{a\theta}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{\Theta}^{\beta\gamma}\,\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}_{\phi\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{8}{\Lambda}\big(\hat{h}_{AB}\,+\,\hat{h}^{*}_{BA}\big)\bigg[\left(\varepsilon^{ab}\,e^{C\,T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{a\alpha}\big)^{*}\right.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,q^{b\beta\,T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(u^{C}_{\gamma\,B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{b}\,\hat{T}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(u^{C\,T}_{\alpha\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(u^{C}_{\alpha\,B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\varepsilon^{ab}\,\varepsilon_{mn}\,\hat{t}^{\dagger}_{ab}\hat{t}^{mn}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\theta\phi}\,e^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(e^{C}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{\Theta}^{\beta\gamma}\,\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}_{\hat{\Theta}\phi}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{ab}\,\varepsilon^{mn}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\theta\phi}\,\left(q^{a\beta\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{m\theta}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{\Delta}^{b\gamma}\,\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}_{n\phi}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\left(\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(u^{C}_{\lambda\,B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{\Theta}^{\beta\gamma}\,\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}_{b\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\varepsilon^{\theta\phi\alpha}\,\left(u^{C\,T}_{\alpha\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(e^{C}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{t}^{ab}\,\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}_{\theta\phi}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon_{ab}\,\varepsilon^{mn}\,\varepsilon^{\eta\theta\phi}\,\left(u^{C\,T}_{\eta\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{m\theta}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{t}^{ab}\,\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}_{n\phi}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\theta\phi}\,\varepsilon^{mn}\,\left(e^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{m\theta}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{\Theta}^{\beta\gamma}\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}_{n\phi}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\nu^{C\,T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(\nu^{C}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{H}^{a}\,\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{a}+\hat{T}^{\alpha}\,\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon^{ab}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\left(\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(u^{C}_{\gamma\,B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{H}^{\dagger}\,\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}_{b\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{b\alpha}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{H}^{\dagger}_{a}\,\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}_{\beta\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{ab}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\left(\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\big(q^{a\beta}_{B}\big)^{*}\right)\,\left(\hat{T}^{\dagger}_{\alpha}\,\hat{\Delta}^{\dagger}_{b\gamma}\right)\;+\;\rm{H.c.}.

The relations provided in (
2.45
), (
2.47
), and (
2.50
) collectively illustrate the decomposition of non-renormalisable interaction shown in Eq. (
2.40
) from the integration of dimension
10
irrep, which may be either scalar or fermionic. These expressions involve coupling two SM fermions and two scalars and respecting the SM gauge symmetry. These higher dimensional terms contribute not only to mass matrices of charged fermions but also to neutral fermions. Additionally, it can generate the Majorana mass of right-handed neutrinos. Furthermore, some pairs have diquark, leptoquark and dilepton couplings. Consequently, these pairs of scalars could be constrained by different phenomena. On similar lines, one can also decompose the expression given in Eq. (
2.40
) using irreps other than
10
-dimensional irreps, which will be shown in Chapter (
4
).

2.7
Conclusion

I
N THIS CHAPTER, we emphasised that
SU(5)
and
SO(10)
are the potential groups for grand unification and discussed that certain irreps are crucial for meeting elementary requirements. These irreps contribute to the Yukawa Lagrangian at both renormalisable and non-renormalisable levels. These irreps contain diverse scalars that are charged under the
SU(3)_{\mathrm{C}}\times SU(2)_{\mathrm{L}}\times U(1)_{\mathrm{Y}}
gauge symmetry, resulting in various types of vertices (leptoquark, diquark and dilepton) with SM fermions. In this chapter, only specific examples of the decomposition of couplings from an
SO(10)
invariant Lagrangian were considered, and the same methodology would be used to evaluate the couplings of other irreps in subsequent chapters. With the knowledge of these various couplings, we can study and analyse different novel predictions and constrain these new scalars from the same.

Chapter 3
Scalar Induced Proton Decays in Renormalisable GUTs

3.1
Overview

U
NIFIED GAUGE THEORY based on a simple group, as we have seen in the previous Chapter (
2
), offers a plethora of scalar fields which yield vertices that are absent in the SM. Such vertices often lead to phenomena that serve as the primary basis for the detectability and verifiability of the GUT. One such novel phenomena is the proton decay.

In Chapter (
2
), we have outlined the technique to compute the SM invariant coupling stemming from
SO(10)
invariant Lagrangian. In this chapter, we begin with computing the resulting effective couplings due to the incorporation of scalar irreps in the Yukawa sector
(i.e.\,120_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\text{and}\,\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}})
in section (
3.2
). Consequently, we use these computed couplings to compute the nucleon decay. Before that, we discuss distinct proton decay modes appearing at even and odd mass dimensions in section (
). Based on the same discussion, in sections (
) and (
), we evaluate
D=6
and
D=7
mass-dimension operators from different irreps capable of destabilising a proton. We relate the computed operators in terms of quarks and leptons to the decay widths of hadrons in section (
) and compute the pattern of leading proton decay modes in a realistic
SO(10)
model in section (
). We compare our study of scalar-mediated proton decays with the known-conventional gauge boson-mediated proton decays in section (
3.3
) and conclude this chapter by outlining the peculiarities of the scalar-mediated proton decays in section (
3.4
).

3.2
Relevant Effective Couplings

In the subsection (
2.4.2
), we explored the interactions between the
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and the
\mathbf{16}
-fermion multiplet. We found that the
T
and
\overline{T}
have leptoquark and diquark couplings capable of destabilising nucleons. The scalar multiplets
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
126_{{\mathrm{H}}}
are also capable of interacting with the
\mathbf{16}
-plet, as indicated by the Eq. (
(iii)
). We proceed to derive the couplings of the various scalar fields residing in
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
with the SM fermions unified in
\mathbf{16}
fermion plet.

3.2.1
\mathbf{16}-\mathbf{16}-120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
couplings

We consider the Yukawa interactions of three indexed antisymmetric tensor,

120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, with

-plet of

whose parameterisation is shown below

-{\cal L}^{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}=G_{AB}\,{\bf 16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,{\bf 16}_{B}\,{120}_{H}\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

is anti-symmetric coupling unlike the case of

\displaystyle{}120_{{\mathrm{H}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\left(5,2\right)\oplus\rm{c.c}\right\}\oplus\left\{\left(10,-6\right)\oplus\rm{c.c}\right\}\oplus\left\{\left(45,2\right)\oplus\rm{c.c}\right\}.

(3.2)

One can decompose Eq. (

) into

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\,G_{AB}\,\Big[-2\,\mathbf{1}{{}^{T}_{A}}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{pB}\,5^{p}_{\mathrm{H}}\,-\mathbf{10}^{pq\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}{{}_{pB}}\,\overline{5}_{q\,{\mathrm{H}}}\,

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}{{}^{T}_{pA}}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}_{qB}\,10^{pq}_{\mathrm{H}}-\sqrt{2}\,\mathbf{1}{{}^{T}_{A}}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{10}^{pq}_{B}\,\overline{10}_{pq\,{\mathrm{H}}}\,

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\,\varepsilon_{pqrst}\,\mathbf{10}^{pq\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{10}^{tu}_{B}\,45^{rs}_{u\,{\mathrm{H}}}\,+\sqrt{2}\,\mathbf{\overline{5}}{{}^{T}_{pA}}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{10}^{qr}_{B}\,\overline{45}^{p}_{qr\,{\mathrm{H}}}\Big]\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.}.

The
5_{{\mathrm{H}}}

(\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}})
and
45_{{\mathrm{H}}}

(\overline{45}_{{\mathrm{H}}})
include a scalar field that is a singlet under

and a triplet under

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/5_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle-i\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}}\,G_{AB}\,\left(-\varepsilon_{ab}\,\nu_{A}^{C\,T}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l_{B}^{a}\,D^{b}+\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\alpha B}T^{\alpha}\right)\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/\overline{5}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle-i\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\,G_{AB}\,\bigg[\left(\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\beta B}+\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\gamma\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{b}_{B}\right)\overline{T}_{1\gamma}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\left(q^{a\alpha\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{B}\,+\,e^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l_{B}^{a}\right)\,\overline{D}_{a}\bigg]\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

The canonically normalised decomposition of
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
is analogous to the decomposition of

done in Eq. (

).
When decomposed, the coupling of

and

of

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle-i2\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,G_{AB}\,\Big(\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,l^{a\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\alpha\,A}\,\Delta^{b\alpha}\,-d^{CT}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\beta B}\,\overline{\Theta}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{ab}\,l^{a\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,l^{b}_{B}\Big)\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/\overline{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle-i2\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,G_{AB}\,\Big[-2\,\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,e^{C}_{B}\,s+\sqrt{2}\,\nu^{C}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{a\,\alpha}_{B}\,\overline{\Delta}_{a\alpha}

(3.5)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\nu^{C\,T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\alpha\,B}\,\Theta_{\beta\gamma}\Big]\;+\;\rm{H.c.}

We provide a procedure for the decomposition and canonical normalisation of
\overline{45}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
in
SU(5)
. The tensor
\overline{45}
is completely antisymmetric in its lower two indices. Below, we write all possible combinations in which one can place
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
indices in
\overline{45}
;

\displaystyle{}\overline{45}^{p}_{qr}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{45}^{a}_{bc}\oplus\overline{45}^{a}_{\alpha b}\oplus\overline{45}^{\alpha}_{ab}\oplus\overline{45}^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\oplus\overline{45}^{\alpha}_{\beta\gamma}.

(3.6)

To begin with, we can write the following tensor as a sum of another traceless tensor and its trace part, as shown below;

\displaystyle{}\overline{45}^{a}_{bc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{1}\left(\delta_{a}^{b}\overline{D}_{c}-\delta^{a}_{c}\overline{D}_{b}\right)

\displaystyle\overline{45}^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}+c_{2}\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\,\overline{D}_{a}.

(3.7)

with
O
being the traceless tensor by definition. The tracelessness condition yields the following;

\displaystyle\overline{45}^{a}_{bc}\,\delta^{b}_{a}+\overline{45}^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}\,\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}=0\hskip 28.45274pt\Rightarrow c_{1}=-3c_{2}.

For canonically normalised
\overline{45}
kinetic term we have,

\displaystyle{}\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}\overline{45}^{a}_{bc}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}\overline{45}^{a}_{bc}\right)+2\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}\overline{45}^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}\overline{45}^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}\right).

(3.8)

The additional factor of
2
in the above equation is due to the symmetry in the exchange of
SU(3)
and
SU(2)
labels,
i.e.

\beta
and
a
.

\displaystyle{}2\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}O^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}O^{\alpha}_{\beta\,a}\right)+24\,c_{2}^{2}\left(\partial_{\dot{\mu}}\overline{D}_{a}\right)^{\dagger}\,\left(\partial^{\dot{\mu}}\overline{D}_{a}\right)

(3.9)

Hence
O\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}O
and
c_{2}\to\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}
for a canonically normalised kinetic term.

Similarly, other decomposed irreps can be canonically normalised, giving us the following result
[
Patel:2022wya
]
.

\displaystyle\overline{45}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle S^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\left(\delta^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\overline{T}_{\beta}-\delta^{\gamma}_{\beta}\overline{T}_{\alpha}\right)\,,~~\overline{45}^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\Omega^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\,\,,

\displaystyle\overline{45}^{\beta}_{\alpha a}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\overline{O}^{\beta}_{\alpha a}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}\delta^{\beta}_{\alpha}\overline{D}_{a}\,,~~\overline{45}^{\beta}_{ab}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon_{ab}{\cal T}^{\beta}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{45}^{a}_{b\alpha}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\overline{\mathbb{T}}^{a}_{b\alpha}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta^{a}_{b}\overline{T}_{\alpha}\,,~~\overline{45}^{c}_{ab}\equiv-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}\left(\delta^{c}_{a}\overline{D}_{b}-\delta^{c}_{b}\overline{D}_{a}\right)\,.

(3.10)

The couplings of the remaining fields present in the
45_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{45}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
-plets can be straightforwardly computed using the decomposition provided in Eq. (
3.2.1
). We obtain:

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{{120_{{\mathrm{H}}}}/45_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle i\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\,G_{AB}\,\bigg[2\,u^{C\,T}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,e^{C}_{B}\,T_{1}^{\alpha}\,+\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\beta B}\,\overline{{\cal T}}_{\gamma}\Big.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big.\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{\alpha aT}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{\beta c}_{B}\,\mathbb{T}^{b\gamma}_{c}\,-\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,e^{CT}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{\gamma a}_{B}\,\overline{\Omega}^{\alpha\beta}_{a}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,u^{C\,T}_{\alpha\,A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{b\beta}_{B}\,O^{\alpha\,a}_{\beta}\begin{comment}\par\par

The crucial inferences drawn from Tabs. (
3.1
, and
3.2
) are listed as follows:

(i)

In scalar-induced proton decay, the proton predominantly decays into channels involving
K^{0}\mu^{+}
or
K^{+}\overline{\nu}
.

(ii)

Additionally, the branching ratio
{\rm BR}[p\to e^{+}\pi^{0}]
is significantly less than
{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\pi^{0}]
.

(iii)

When
\overline{T}
is lighter than
T
, the proton mainly decays through channels that involve charged mesons and neutrinos. Since
T
does not couple to neutrinos, decays mediated by
T
typically result in neutral mesons and charged leptons.

(iv)

A comparison between Tabs. (
3.1
and
3.2
) indicates that the decay patterns of the proton are not significantly influenced by whether the lightest
T
and
\overline{T}
resides in
{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
or
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. This suggests that the qualitative results would remain consistent even if the lightest pairs,
T
and
\overline{T}
, are general linear combinations of triplets and anti-triplets respectively, from
{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

To check the robustness of the above observations, we evaluate the proton decay branching ratios for not only the best-fit solution but also for several other solutions with acceptable
\chi^{2}
values at the minimum, determined in
[
Mummidi:2021anm
]
. The results are displayed in Figs. (
3.1
and (
3.2
).

Figure 3.1:
The scalar-mediated proton decay spectrum within the framework of the minimal renormalisable non-supersymmetric
SO(10)
model. Various branching ratios have been calculated under the assumption that the lightest pair of colour triplets,
T
and
\overline{T}
, originate from
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

Figure 3.2:
Similar to Fig. (
3.1
), but with the assumption that the lightest triplets predominantly originate from
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

From the Figs. (
3.1
and
3.2
), it is evident that the predictions for various branching ratios as listed in Tabs. (
3.1
and (
3.2
) remain consistent across other viable solutions as well. In Figs. (
3.1
and
3.2
), frequency represents the probability of the event in the solution space. The key characteristic of the proton decay spectrum, as referenced in (
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
, and
(iv)
), can be explained by the flavour structure of the model. The realistic fermion mass spectrum in the underlying model results in hierarchical structures for the matrices
H
and
F
, which are given as follows:

H\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{1}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{7}&0&0\\
0&\lambda^{3}&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right)\,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\,~~F\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{5}&\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}\\
\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\end{array}\right)\,,

where,
\lambda=0.23
represents the Cabibbo angle, and we have omitted coefficients of order one for simplicity in front of each element. It is important to mention the branching ratios listed in both the Tabs. (
3.1
and
3.2
) are independent of the uncertain parameter
\alpha_{1,2}
mentioned in Eq. (
3.2.2
). The Yukawa matrices for the charged fermions—
Y_{u}
,
Y_{d}
, and
Y_{e}
—are derived from linear combinations of
H
and
F
. The unitary matrices that diagonalise these Yukawa matrices and the neutrino mass matrix typically exhibit the following generic form:

U_{u}\sim U_{d}\sim U_{e}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\lambda&\lambda^{3}\\
\lambda&1&\lambda^{2}\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&1\end{array}\right)\,,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\,~~U_{\nu}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}&&\\
&{\cal O}(1)&\\
&&\end{array}\right)\,

where
U_{\nu}
is matrix having all the elements of
{\cal O}(1)
.
U_{u,d,e}
could taken as
CKM
-type, leading to small quark mixing while the large mixing in the PMNS matrix arises through
U_{\nu}
. From Eqs. (
3.2.2
and
3.2.2
), we find

\displaystyle U_{f}^{T}\,H\,U_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{1}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{5}&\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}\\
\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&1\end{array}\right)\,,~~U_{f}^{T}\,F\,U_{f^{\prime}}\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{5}&\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}\\
\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\end{array}\right)\,,

\displaystyle U_{\nu}^{T}\,H\,U_{d}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{1}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&1\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&1\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&1\end{array}\right)\,,~~U_{\nu}^{T}\,F\,U_{d}\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\end{array}\right)\,.

for every
f,f^{\prime}=u,d,e
.

Incorporating these results into the Eqs. (
,
, and
B.1
), we arrive at the following

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma[p\to e_{i}^{+}\,\pi^{0}]}{\Gamma[p\to e_{i}^{+}\,K^{0}]}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m{{}^{2}_{\pi^{0}}})^{2}}{(m_{p}^{2}-m{{}^{2}_{K^{0}}})^{2}}\,\frac{(1+\tilde{D}+\tilde{F})^{2}}{2\left(1+\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}(\tilde{D}-\tilde{F})\right)^{2}}\,{\lambda}^{2}\simeq 3\lambda^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma[p\to\overline{\nu}\,\pi^{+}]}{\Gamma[p\to\overline{\nu}\,K^{+}]}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m{{}^{2}_{\pi^{+}}})^{2}}{(m_{p}^{2}-m{{}^{2}_{K^{+}}})^{2}}\,\frac{(1+\tilde{D}+\tilde{F})^{2}}{\left(1+\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}(\tilde{D}+\tilde{F})\right)^{2}}\,{\lambda}^{2}\simeq 2\lambda^{2}\,.

(3.58)

which explains the observation listed as point
(i)
above. Further, point
(ii)
can be explained as

\frac{\Gamma[p\to e^{+}\,\pi^{0}]}{\Gamma[p\to\mu^{+}\,\pi^{0}]}\simeq\frac{\Gamma[p\to e^{+}\,K^{0}]}{\Gamma[p\to\mu^{+}\,K^{0}]}\simeq\lambda^{2}\,,

(3.59)

Furthermore, point
(iii)
can be explained from the following.

\frac{\Gamma[p\to\mu^{+}\,K^{0}]}{\Gamma[p\to\overline{\nu}\,K^{+}]}\simeq\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m{{}^{2}_{K^{0}}})^{2}}{(m_{p}^{2}-m{{}^{2}_{K^{+}}})^{2}}\,\frac{\left(1+\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}(\tilde{D}-\tilde{F})\right)^{2}}{\left(1+\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}(\tilde{D}+\tilde{F})\right)^{2}}\,\frac{4{\lambda}^{2}}{9}\simeq 0.2\lambda^{2}\,.

(3.60)

Additionally, it is evident from Eq. (
3.2.2
) that the flavour structure of the couplings relevant to proton decay is similar for both
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. This similarity supports the observations noted in
(iv)
.

3.2.3
Constraint on the Masses of
T
and
\overline{T}

Finally, by utilising the current experimental limits on the proton decay lifetime for various channels, we derive the stringent limits on the masses of
T
and
\overline{T}
within the realistic model being considered. When the lightest
T
primarily originates from
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, the stringent limit on its mass is imposed by the decay channels that involve
\mu^{+}
. We find:

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\,K^{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.6\times 10^{33}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{0.1}\right)^{4}\times\left(\frac{M_{T}}{1.4\times 10^{11}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{4}\,,

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\,\pi^{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.6\times 10^{34}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{0.1}\right)^{4}\times\left(\frac{M_{T}}{1.3\times 10^{11}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{4}\,,

provided, the first factor on the right-hand side represents the current experimental lower bounds on the lifetime of proton decay in the respective channels. Similarly, when
\overline{T}
predominantly originates from
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, the most stringent upper bound on its mass is derived from the proton decay channels into neutrinos and
K^{+}
:

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\overline{\nu}\,K^{+}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 5.9\times 10^{33}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{0.1}\right)^{4}\times\left(\frac{M_{\overline{T}}}{6.4\times 10^{11}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{4}.\,

(3.62)

The experimental limits on the lifetimes for various proton decay channels referenced in the above calculations are taken from
[
Super-Kamiokande:2020wjk
,
Super-Kamiokande:2012zik
,
Super-Kamiokande:2014otb
]
. These studies provide the current lower bounds on the lifetime of the proton at
90\,\%
confidence level.

If the lightest
T
and
\overline{T}
arise dominantly from
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, we find

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\,K^{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.6\times 10^{33}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{0.1}\right)^{4}\times\left(\frac{M_{T_{i}}}{2.5\times 10^{10}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{4}\,,

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\,\pi^{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.6\times 10^{34}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{0.1}\right)^{4}\times\left(\frac{M_{T_{i}}}{2.7\times 10^{10}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{4},\,

(3.63)

and

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\overline{\nu}\,K^{+}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 5.9\times 10^{33}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{0.1}\right)^{4}\times\left(\frac{M_{\overline{T}}}{1.1\times 10^{11}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{4}.\,

(3.64)

It can be noticed that the limits on the masses of
T
and
\overline{T}
are slightly lower than the ones given in Eqs. (
3.2.3
and
3.62
). This is due to the fact that the magnitude of Yukawa couplings with
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
are somewhat smaller than those with
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

3.2.4
Constraint on the Mass of
\Delta

In addition to the
D=6
operators previously discussed in section (
), a
D=7
operator emerges within the model due to
B-L
violation and mixing between
\overline{T}
and
\Delta
, as outlined in section (
). This operator induces proton decay modes such as
p\to\nu\,\pi^{+}
and
p\to\nu\,K^{+}
. Given that the mass of
\overline{T}
is already constrained by the
D=6
operators, the lower bound on the mass of
\Delta
can be deduced. To calculate this, we assume the quartic coupling
\lambda=1
,
\alpha_{2}=0.1
,
v_{\overline{D}}=174
GeV, and
M_{\overline{T}}=1.1\times 10^{11}
GeV, as specified in Eq. (
3.2.4
). Substituting these parameters into Eqs. (
and
), we determine:

\displaystyle\tau/{\rm BR}[p\to\nu\,K^{+}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 5.9\times 10^{33}\,{\rm yrs}\,\times\left(\frac{10^{11}\,{\rm GeV}}{v_{\sigma}}\right)^{2}\times\left(\frac{M_{\Delta}}{7.0\times 10^{6}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{2}.

The calculated lower bound on
M_{\Delta}
turns out to be two orders of magnitude lower than that reported in
[
Babu:2012vb
]
for the same
B-L
breaking scale. This difference is due to the values of the Yukawa couplings used, as well as an additional factor of
(4/15)^{2}
given in Eq. (
), which stems from the decomposition.

3.3
Gauge Boson Mediated Proton Decay

To clearly distinguish between scalar-mediated and gauge boson-mediated proton decay, we calculate the gauge-mediated proton decay partial widths for a quantitative comparison. The currents associated with
B-L
charged gauge bosons,
X\sim(3,2,-5/6)
and
Y\sim(3,2,1/6)
, which belong to the adjoint representation of
SO(10)
, are detailed as follows
1
1

1

There is also a gauge boson with SM charges
Z\sim(3,1,2/3)
having a
B-L
charge of
4/3
. However, it does not mediate proton decay by itself
[
Buchmuller:2019ipg
]
.

[
Machacek:1979tx
]
.
The interactions of the
X
and
Y
gauge bosons with SM fermions can be inferred from the Eq. (
2.38
), where
L_{AB}\,=\,g_{10}\,\delta_{AB}
;

\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{X\&Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\,g_{10}\,\Big[\sqrt{2}\,\overline{X}_{\dot{\mu}}\left(q^{\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,u^{C}_{A}+e^{C\,\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,q_{A}-d^{C\,\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,l_{A}\right)\Big.

(3.66)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big.2\,\overline{Y}_{\dot{\mu}}\left(q^{\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,d^{C}_{A}+\nu^{C\,\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,q_{A}-u^{C\,\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,l_{A}\right)\Big]\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\,,

where
g_{10}
is unified gauge coupling and we have supressed the
SU(3)
and
SU(2)
indices for brevity. Integrating out the degrees of freedom of
X
and
Y
bosons using classical equations of motion, the relevant effective operators for the proton decay are as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm eff}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{2M_{X}^{2}}\left(u^{C\,\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,q_{A}\right)\,\,\left(e^{C\,\dagger}_{B}\,\overline{\sigma}_{\dot{\mu}}\,q_{B}-d^{C\,\dagger}_{B}\overline{\sigma}_{\dot{\mu}}l_{B}\right)

(3.67)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{2M_{Y}^{2}}\left(d^{C\,\dagger}_{A}\,\overline{\sigma}^{\dot{\mu}}\,q_{A}\right)\,\,\left(u^{C\,\dagger}_{B}\,\overline{\sigma}_{\dot{\mu}}\,l_{B}\right)\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\,.

Using Fierz relations for two-component Weyl spinors
[
Dreiner:2008tw
]
and transforming into the physical basis,
f\to U_{f}f
, we obtain, (cf.
[
Buchmuller:2019ipg
]
for details)

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm eff}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k[u_{A},d_{B},e^{C}_{C},u^{C}_{D}]\left(\overline{e^{C}}_{C}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{D}\,u_{A}\,d_{B}\right)

(3.68)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle k[u^{C}_{A},d^{C}_{B},e_{C},u_{D}]\left(\overline{d^{C}}_{B}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{A}\,u_{D}\,e_{C}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle k[u^{C}_{A},d^{C}_{B},\nu_{C},d_{D}]\left(\overline{d^{C}}_{B}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{A}\,d_{D}\,\nu_{C}\right)\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.},,

with

\displaystyle k[u_{A},d_{B},e^{C}_{C},u^{C}_{D}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{M_{X}^{2}}\bigg[\left(U_{e^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{d}\right)_{CB}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{u}\right)_{DA}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\;\left(U_{e^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{u}\right)_{CA}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{d}\right)_{DB}\bigg]\,,

\displaystyle k[u^{C}_{A},d^{C}_{B},e_{C},u_{D}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{M_{X}^{2}}\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{e}\right)_{BC}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{u}\right)_{AD}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\;\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{M_{Y}^{2}}\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{u}\right)_{BD}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{e}\right)_{AC}\,,

\displaystyle k[u^{C}_{A},d^{C}_{B},\nu_{C},d_{D}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{M_{X}^{2}}\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{\nu}\right)_{BC}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{d}\right)_{AD}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\;\;\frac{g_{10}^{2}}{M_{Y}^{2}}\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{d}\right)_{BD}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}U_{\nu}\right)_{AC}.\,

The decay widths of the proton into various channels can be calculated by setting
y[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}]=k[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}]
and
y^{\prime}[f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4}]=0
, using the expressions given in the Eqs. (
, and
B.1
). The branching pattern for the leading decay modes of the proton has been computed from the best-fit solution, assuming different mass hierarchies among the
X
and
Y

3.3
). Additionally, the spectrum of branching ratios for several solutions with acceptable
\chi^{2}

Table 3.3:
Proton decay branching fractions estimated for the best fit solution for various hierarchies among the masses of
X
and
Y
gauge bosons in case of the minimal renormalizable non-supersymmetric
SO(10)
model.

Figure 3.3:
The spectrum of proton decay induced by gauge bosons for several solutions within the same
SO(10)
model as in Figs. (
3.1
and
3.2
).

It is evident from Tab. (
3.3
) and Fig. (
3.3
) that the branching pattern of gauge boson-mediated proton decay is entirely different from the scalar field-induced proton decay. The important differences are outlined as follows:

(i)

In the case of gauge boson mediation the proton dominantly decays into
\pi^{0}\,e^{+}
or
\pi^{+}\overline{\nu}
, while the scalar-induced proton decay favours the channels involving
K^{0}\,\mu^{+}
or
K^{+}\,\overline{\nu}
.

(ii)

Typically, for the case of scalar mediated proton decay
{\rm BR}[p\to e^{+}\pi^{0}]\ll{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\pi^{0}]
. This is in contrast to the gauge boson-mediated decays in which one typically finds
{\rm BR}[p\to e^{+}\pi^{0}]\gg{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\pi^{0}]
. This difference is primarily due to the hierarchical nature of the Yukawa couplings, which are typically larger for the second generation fermions than the first. In contrast, the gauge parameter couples uniformly across all generations of fermions.

3.4
Summary

P
ROTON DECAY IS one of the crucial phenomena which can validify GUTs and offers a glimpse into high-energy phenomena from a low-energy. Observing proton decay would definitively discard the conservation of the baryon number. Additionally, it could shed light on the nature of the theories valid at ultra-violet scales, where violations of baryon number and lepton number originate. This understanding necessitates calculating nucleon decay widths within specific models, considering all potential sources of its decay.

In this chapter, we consider the most general scalar spectrum possible in the renormalisable
SO(10)
Yukawa sector and proceed to explicitly compute the couplings of various scalars that can lead to baryon and lepton number-violating decays of hadrons at the tree level. We calculate effective operators by integrating out the scalar fields while also considering the possibility of mixing between the scalars within the same GUT multiplet. We evaluated
D=6
operators that conserve baryon-minus-lepton number (
B-L
) and
D=7
operators which violate
B-L
. Thereafter, we quantify the proton decay widths using these operators and offer a detailed analysis of scalar-mediated proton decay within a realistic GUT model based on
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

Key highlights from our comprehensive analysis are outlined as follows:

•

The irreps capable of contributing to the renormalisable
SO(10)
Yukawa sector,
i.e.

10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
,
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, consists of different scalar fields which are charged under
B-L
, but not all of them can induce proton decay. The
D=6
operators induced baryon decays originate from only three pairs of colour triplet fields:
T(3,1,-\frac{1}{3})
,
{\cal T}(3,1,-\frac{4}{3})
, and
\mathbb{T}(3,3,-\frac{1}{3})
along with their conjugate partners.

•

In the class of realistic models based on
{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and/or
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, only
T
and
\overline{T}
induce the nucleon decay at the tree level. Additionally,
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
contains
{\cal T}
and
\mathbb{T}
fields, it only exhibits leptoquark vertex.

•

When the Yukawa sector has
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, the contribution to proton decay from
{\cal T}
-
\overline{\cal T}
vanishes at the tree level due to the anti-symmetric nature of the Yukawa couplings. Consequently, these fields can only contribute to proton decay at the loop level.

•

The
B+L
conserving nucleon decays is induced by
D=7
operators at the leading order, and is mediated by
\Theta(3,1,\frac{2}{3})
,
\Delta(3,2,\frac{1}{6})
,
\Omega(3,2,\frac{7}{6})
and their conjugate partners. In the
SO(10)
models devoid of
{120}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, only
\Delta
can induce such decays.

In a minimal model based on
{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, the proton decay spectrum observations are different when scalar-mediated contributions are dominant and have been discussed in the section (
). The primary source of distinction stems from the nature of Yukawa and gauge couplings. Yukawa couplings are more sensitive to the flavour structure of the underlying GUT model. Consequently, proton decay primarily results in
\overline{\nu}\,K^{+}
or
\mu^{+}\,K^{0}
if
\overline{T}
or
T
is lighter, respectively. Additionally, it is noted that
{\rm BR}[p\to\mu^{+}\pi^{0}]
is significantly greater than
{\rm BR}[p\to e^{+}\pi^{0}]
. These patterns contrast with those of gauge boson-mediated proton decays, where protons typically decay into
\overline{\nu}\,\pi^{+}
or
e^{+}\,\pi^{0}
and the branching ratio for
\mu^{+}
is much lower than for
e^{+}
. Therefore, if scalar-mediated contributions predominate, they can offer valuable insights into the Yukawa structure of the theory, significantly influencing the predicted proton decay patterns.

Chapter 4
Scalar Induced Proton Decays in Non-Renormalisable GUTs

4.1
Overview

N
ON-RENORMALISABLE GRAND unified model with irreps contributing to the Yukawa sector having fewer degrees of freedom offer alternative solutions to the inconsistencies found in renormalisable GUT models in the UV regime, as discussed in Chapter (
2
). In this chapter, our focus is to study non-renormalisable
SO(10)
Yukawa interactions in which LTR are absent. We consider
SO(10)
models constructed from irreps with less degree of freedoms and couples at the non-renormalisable level.
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
is one such irrep and we evaluated the coupling of
\mathbf{16}
with
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
in Chapter (
2
). As many
B-L
charged scalars reside in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
(cf. Tab. (
2.3
)), they can induce proton decays. Analogous to Chapter (
3
), we constrain the scalars residing in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
from proton decays in this chapter.

In Section (
4.2
), we continue the analysis of the couplings between
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\mathbf{16}
, initiated in Chapter (
2
). We compute the contributions from various leptoquark and diquark couplings, generated by different scalar pairs, to
B-L
conserving two-body proton decay modes in section (
4.3
). In the same section, we also discuss some general characteristics observed from the evaluated coefficients. We argue that
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
can assume a role similar to that of
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
in renormalisable
SO(10)
GUTs, as detailed in section (
4.4
), and utilise the evaluated strengths of various operators to compute the branching pattern of proton decay in a realistic
SO(10)
model based on
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. Key aspects of proton decay, including the leading decay mode and constraints on various pairs of tree-level proton decay mediators, are highlighted, and the study is concluded in section (
).
