\displaystyle-{\cal L}^{\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}_{Y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{AB}\,\mathbf{16}^{T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,\mathbf{16}_{B}\,\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\sqrt{15}}\,F_{AB}\,\Big(2\,d^{CT}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\beta B}\,\Sigma^{\alpha\beta}\,-\,\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\gamma A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\sigma B}\,{S}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\Big.

\displaystyle\Big.+\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\sigma A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\gamma B}\,{\cal S}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\,+\,\sqrt{2}\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\alpha T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{\sigma c}_{B}\,\mathbb{S}^{\beta\gamma b}_{\sigma c}\Big)\,

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\rm{H.c.}\,.

Here,
F
is symmetric in the flavour space. As it is evident,
\overline{S}
and
\mathbb{S}
interact with left-chiral quark fields, while the other coloured sextets couple with right-chiral quarks.

In the
SO(10)
models having irreps
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
together, the fields
S
and
\tilde{S}
can mix with each other through gauge-invariant terms such as
\left(\,120_{{\mathrm{H}}}\right.

\,\left.\overline{{126}}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,45_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\right)
or
\left(\,120_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\overline{126}^{\dagger}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,210_{{\mathrm{H}}}\,\right)
. The resulting physical states are combinations of
S
and
\tilde{S}
. Further, we assume that this mixing is characterised by real parameters;

S_{1}=S\,\cos\theta\,+\,\tilde{S}\,\sin\theta\,,~~S_{2}=-S\,\sin\theta\,\,+\,\tilde{S}\,\cos\theta\,\,.

(5.9)

We define
c_{\theta}\equiv\cos\theta
and
s_{\theta}\equiv\sin\theta
which will be used in further sections. Additionally, the linear combination
S_{1}
is assumed to be the lighter mass eigenstate,
i.e.

M_{S_{1}}<M_{S_{2}}
.

After substituting
S
and
\tilde{S}
with the physical states
S_{1,2}
as described in Eq. (
5.9
), and transforming the quark fields into their physical basis by transforming
f\to U_{f}f
, the Yukawa couplings between the various sextet fields
\Phi=\Sigma,S_{i},{\cal S},\mathbb{S},\overline{S}
, and the quarks can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle-{\cal L}_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Y^{\Sigma}_{AB}\,d^{CT}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\beta B}\,\Sigma^{\alpha\beta}\,+\,Y^{S_{i}}_{AB}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\gamma A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\sigma B}\,{S_{i}}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\,

(5.10)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle Y^{\cal S}_{AB}\,\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\sigma A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C}_{\gamma B}\,{\cal S}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\,+\,Y^{\mathbb{S}}_{AB}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\epsilon_{ab}\,q^{a\alpha T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q^{\sigma c}_{B}\,\mathbb{S}^{\beta\gamma b}_{\sigma c}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle Y^{\overline{S}}_{AB}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{\gamma T}_{A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{\sigma}_{B}\,\overline{S}^{\alpha\beta}_{\sigma}\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

where
i=1,2
. The
3\times 3
matrices
Y^{\Phi}
obtained from Eqs. (
5.7
and
5.8
) are given as:

\displaystyle Y^{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2i}{\sqrt{15}}\,U_{d^{C}}^{T}\,F\,U_{d^{C}}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{S_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2}i}{\sqrt{3}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}c_{\theta}\,U_{u^{C}}^{T}\,F\,U_{d^{C}}\,-\,\sqrt{2}s_{\theta}\,U_{u^{C}}^{T}\,G\,U_{d^{C}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle Y^{S_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{2}i}{\sqrt{3}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}s_{\theta}\,U_{u^{C}}^{T}\,F\,U_{d^{C}}\,+\,\sqrt{2}c_{\theta}\,U_{u^{C}}^{T}\,G\,U_{d^{C}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle Y^{\cal S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\sqrt{15}}\,U_{u^{C}}^{T}\,F\,U_{u^{C}}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sqrt{2}i}{\sqrt{15}}\,U_{q}^{T}\,F\,U_{q^{\prime}}\,,

\displaystyle Y^{\overline{S}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2i}{\sqrt{3}}\,U_{u}^{T}\,G\,U_{d}\,.

(5.11)

Here,
q,q^{\prime}=u,d
. The matrices
Y^{\Sigma}
and
Y^{\cal S}
are symmetric in the flavor space, while
Y^{\mathbb{S}}
is symmetric when
q=q^{\prime}
. It is important to note that the matrices
U_{u}
and
U_{d}
determine the quark mixing matrix, defined as
U_{u}^{\dagger}U_{d}\equiv V_{\rm CKM}
. Thus, assuming
U_{u}\approx U_{d}
is a reasonable approximation, and at the leading order,
Y^{\mathbb{S}}
can be considered symmetric.

The advantage of formulating the expressions in Eq. (
5.2
) is that all the
Y^{\Phi}
matrices can be explicitly calculated within realistic
SO(10)
models where the fundamental couplings
F
,
G
, and the diagonalising matrices
U_{f}
are derived from fits to the fermion mass data as mentioned in section (
). In subsequent sections, we will employ these couplings to analyse various phenomenologically significant processes that only involve sextet scalars: quark flavour violation, neutron-antineutron oscillation and baryogenesis.

5.3
Quark flavour violation

The
Y^{\Phi}
shown in Eq. (
5.2
) depends on the Yukawa couplings
G
and
F
. Generally,
G
and
F
are not diagonal in generation space, allowing sextet scalars to introduce a new source of flavour violation in the quark sector. Additionally,
Y^{\Phi}
depends on unitary matrices
(U_{u,u^{C},d,d^{C}})
that diagonalise charged fermion mass matrices. This rotation from flavour to physical basis can also act as a flavour violation, making a diagonal Yukawa matrix non-diagonal. The most stringent constraints on this type of new physics come from
|\Delta F|=2
processes, which involve neutral meson-antimeson oscillations
[
Buras:2003jf
,
Buras:2005xt
,
Buras:2020xsm
,
Antonelli:2009ws
]
. Following effective theory analysis, we assess the contributions from sextets to
K^{0}-\overline{K}^{0}
,
B^{0}_{d}-\overline{B}^{0}_{d}
,
B^{0}_{s}-\overline{B}^{0}_{s}
, and
D^{0}-\overline{D}^{0}
mixing at both tree and one-loop levels.

Integrating out various sextets scalar fields from Eq. (
5.10
), the effective Lagrangian can be written as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}^{\Delta F=2}_{\rm eff}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{q=d,u}\left(c_{q}\,{\cal{O}}_{q}\;+\;\tilde{c}_{q}\,{\cal{\tilde{O}}}_{q}\right)\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\,,

(5.12)

we find the following independent operators

\displaystyle{\cal{\tilde{O}}}_{d}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Big(\overline{d{{}_{R}^{\alpha}}}_{A}\gamma^{\dot{\mu}}\,d{{}_{R\,C}^{\alpha}}\Big)\,\left(\overline{d{{}_{R}^{\beta}}}_{B}\,\gamma_{\dot{\mu}}\,d{{}_{R\,D}^{\beta}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal{\tilde{O}}}_{u}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Big(\overline{u{{}_{R}^{\alpha}}}_{A}\gamma^{\dot{\mu}}\,u{{}_{R\,C}^{\alpha}}\Big)\,\left(\overline{u{{}_{R}^{\beta}}}_{B}\,\gamma_{\dot{\mu}}\,u{{}_{R\,D}^{\beta}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{d}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Big(\overline{d{{}_{L}^{\alpha}}}_{A}\gamma^{\dot{\mu}}\,d{{}_{L\,C}^{\alpha}}\Big)\,\left(\overline{d{{}_{L}^{\beta}}}_{B}\,\gamma_{\dot{\mu}}\,d{{}_{L\,D}^{\beta}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{u}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Big(\overline{u{{}_{L}^{\alpha}}}_{A}\gamma^{\dot{\mu}}\,u{{}_{L\,C}^{\alpha}}\Big)\,\left(\overline{u{{}_{L}^{\beta}}}_{B}\,\gamma_{\dot{\mu}}\,u{{}_{L\,D}^{\beta}}\right)\,.

(5.13)

Here, we use
q=q_{L}
and
q^{C}=\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,q_{R}^{*}
, for
q=u,d
, to obtain the above operators in the usual left- and right-chiral notations as also done in Chapter (
3
). We have also used two components Fierz transformation rules, given in Eq. (
), to match the integrated out operators with the conventional ones given in Eq. (
5.3
). Further,
{\cal O}_{u,d}
and
{\cal\tilde{O}}_{u,d}
are related by the interchange of
L\leftrightarrow R
chirality. These operators change the flavour by two units and induce flavour-changing neutral meson-antimeson oscillations in the up-type and down-type quark sectors, respectively.

Figure 5.1:
Feynman graph of flavour violation process induced by
\Sigma
, drawn using
[
Harlander:2020cyh
]

We integrate out the heavy degrees of freedom of sextet scalars using the equations of motion in Eq. (
5.10
), resulting in four-fermion operators. These operators are then matched with those given in Eq. (
5.12
), yielding the strength of the operators, as shown below;

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{d}[A,B,C,D]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M_{\Sigma}^{2}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}Y^{\Sigma}_{AB}(Y^{\Sigma}_{CD})^{*}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{6}{64\pi^{2}}\left[\left(Y_{2}^{\Sigma}\right)_{AC}\left(Y_{2}^{\Sigma}\right)_{BD}+\left(Y_{2}^{\Sigma}\right)_{AD}\left(Y_{2}^{\Sigma}\right)_{BC}\right]\bigg]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}_{S_{1}}}\,\frac{6}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{AC}\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{BD}+\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{AD}\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{BC}\right].\,

In the above expression, Eq. (
5.3
),
Y_{2}^{\Phi}=Y^{\Phi}Y^{\Phi\dagger}
and
\bar{Y}_{2}^{\Phi}=Y^{\Phi\dagger}Y^{\Phi}
represent hermitian matrices. The first term indicates the tree-level contribution mediated by
\Sigma
, while the second and third terms represent contributions from 1-loop processes involving the scalars
\Sigma
and
S_{1}
, respectively. It is important to note that the contribution from
S_{2}
has not been included due to the implicit assumption that
M_{S_{2}}>M_{S_{1}}
.

Analogously, we find the following effective strengths of the remaining operators as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{u}[A,B,C,D]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}}\bigg[Y^{\cal S}_{AB}(Y^{\cal S}_{CD})^{*}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{6}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\left(Y_{2}^{\cal S}\right)_{AC}\left(Y_{2}^{\cal S}\right)_{BD}+\left(Y_{2}^{\cal S}\right)_{AD}\left(Y_{2}^{\cal S}\right)_{BC}\right]\bigg]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}_{S_{1}}}\,\frac{6}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\left(Y_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{AC}\left(Y_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{BD}+\left(Y_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{AD}\left(Y_{2}^{S_{1}}\right)_{BC}\right],\,

\displaystyle c_{d}[A,B,C,D]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}}\,\bigg[Y^{\mathbb{S}}_{AB}(Y^{\mathbb{S}}_{CD})^{*}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{30}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{AC}\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{BD}+\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{AD}\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{BC}\bigg]\right]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}_{\overline{S}}}\frac{6}{16\pi^{2}}\,\left[\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{AC}\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{BD}+\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{AD}\left(\bar{Y}_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{BC}\right],\,

and

\displaystyle c_{u}[A,B,C,D]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}}\,\bigg[Y^{\mathbb{S}}_{AB}(Y^{\mathbb{S}}_{CD})^{*}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{30}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{AC}\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{BD}+\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{AD}\left(Y_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\right)_{BC}\right]\bigg]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}_{\overline{S}}}\frac{6}{16\pi^{2}}\,\left[\left(Y_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{AC}\left(Y_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{BD}+\left(Y_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{AD}\left(Y_{2}^{\overline{S}}\right)_{BC}\right].\,

From the given expressions for
\tilde{c}_{u,d}
and
c_{u,d}
, it is evident that the sextet
\Sigma
contributes to flavour violations in the down-type quark sector, while
{\cal S}
exclusively contributes to flavour violation in the up-quark sector. Additionally,
\mathbb{S}
contributes to flavour violations in both up and down quark sectors at the tree level. The contributions from
S_{1}
and
\overline{S}
to flavour violation occur only at the loop level. However, these contributions can be comparable to those at the tree level depending on the hierarchical structure of the Yukawa couplings.

For an estimation of the constraints on the masses and couplings of sextet scalars, it is essential to evolve the relevant strength
c_{u,d}
and
\tilde{c}_{u,d}
from the mass scale of the integrated-out scalar, denoted by
\mu=M_{\Phi}
, down to the scale at which meson-antimeson mixing is experimentally observed. For
K^{0}-\overline{K}^{0}
oscillation, the pertinent scale is
\mu=2
GeV. The renormalisation group equation (RGE) evolved effective coefficient, for cases where
M_{\Phi}>m_{t}
(the mass of the top quark), is calculated as follows
[
Ciuchini:1998ix
]
:

C^{1}_{K}=\left(0.82-0.016\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)^{0.29}\,c_{d}[1,1,2,2](M_{\Phi}).\,

(5.18)

The analogous RGE equation for
\tilde{C}^{1}_{K}
can be derived by substituting
c_{d}
with
\tilde{c}_{d}
in the Eq. (
5.18
). It is important to note that both
C^{1}_{K}
and
\tilde{C}^{1}_{K}
are coefficients for the effective operators,
Q_{1}
and
\tilde{Q}_{1}
, respectively. These operators are listed in
[
Ciuchini:1998ix
]
and correspond to our
{\cal O}_{d}
and
{\cal\tilde{O}}_{d}
, with
A=B=1
and
C=D=2
. These operators do not mix with other operators during RGE evolution, simplifying the running expressions, as shown in Eq. (
5.18
).

Similarly, we can arrive at the expression for Wilson coefficients for
B^{0}_{d,s}
-
\overline{B}^{0}_{d,s}
as follows;

\displaystyle C^{1}_{B_{d}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(0.865-0.017\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)^{0.29}\,c_{d}[1,1,3,3](M_{\Phi})\,,

\displaystyle C^{1}_{B_{s}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(0.865-0.017\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)^{0.29}\,c_{d}[2,2,3,3](M_{\Phi}),\,

(5.19)

at
\mu=m_{b}

[
Becirevic:2001jj
]
. In the case of the charm mixing governed by
D^{0}
-
\overline{D}^{0}
oscillations, one finds
[
UTfit:2007eik
]
,

C^{1}_{D}=\left(0.837-0.016\,\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(m_{t})}\right)^{0.29}\,c_{u}[1,1,2,2](M_{\Phi}),\,

(5.20)

at
\mu=2.8
GeV. Various coefficients then can be compared with the present limits obtained from a fit to the experimental data by UTFit collaboration
[
UTfit:2007eik
]
. The present upper limits on the effective strengths are as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lr}\left|C^{1}_{K}\right|<9.6\times 10^{-13},&\left|C^{1}_{B_{d}}\right|<2.3\times 10^{-11}\\
\left|C^{1}_{B_{s}}\right|<1.1\times 10^{-9},\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}&\left|C^{1}_{D}\right|<7.2\times 10^{-13}.\\
\end{array}

The same upper bounds also apply to the corresponding
\tilde{C}^{1}
. One can constrain the masses of sextet scalars by knowing the bounds on different Wilson coefficients, as provided in Eq. (
5.3
), and Yukawa matrices.

5.4
Neutron-Antineutron Oscillations

The colour sextet fields in renormalisable
SO(10)
models can also induce transitions between neutral baryons and their antiparticles. Unlike the flavour transitions described earlier, this process requires a source of baryon number violation. In
SO(10)
, the source of baryon number violation is inherently present, and such oscillation typically emerges from gauge-invariant quartic couplings involving three sextet fields and an SM singlet from
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
,
i.e.
\sigma
, which has a
B-L
charge of
-2
(refer to Tab. (
2.2
)). The
vev
of
\sigma
not only breaks
B-L
and generates Majorana masses for the right-handed neutrinos, but also leads to
B-L
violating trilinear couplings among various sextet scalars. The
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillations via dimension-nine, electrically neutral and six-fermion operators
[
Rao:1982gt
,
Caswell:1982qs
,
Rao:1983sd
]
and violates baryon number by two units.

To derive the effective operators that mediate neutral baryon-antibaryon oscillations, we begin by constructing the most general quartic interaction terms that involve three sextet scalars and
\sigma
, while respecting the SM gauge symmetry. Such combinations are listed below:

\displaystyle\eta_{ij}\,\sigma\,\Sigma^{\alpha\beta}\,{S_{i}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\gamma}\,{S_{j}}^{\theta}_{\beta\theta}\,

(5.24)

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,\varepsilon_{\alpha\gamma\theta}\,\sigma\,\Sigma^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma^{\gamma\sigma}\,{\cal S}^{\theta}_{\beta\sigma}

(5.25)

\displaystyle\eta_{3}\,\sigma^{*}\,\Sigma^{*}_{\alpha\beta}\,\mathbb{S}^{\alpha\sigma a}_{\sigma b}\,\mathbb{S}^{\beta\rho b}_{\rho a}\,

(5.26)

\displaystyle\eta_{4}\,\sigma^{*}\,\Sigma^{*}_{\alpha\beta}\,\overline{S}^{\alpha\sigma}_{\sigma}\,\overline{S}^{\beta\rho}_{\rho}\,.

(5.27)

The interaction terms listed in the first two lines of the above Eq. (
5.24
) can originate from a quartic term involving four
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
fields,
i.e.

\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and mixing between
S
and
\tilde{S}
. The interaction terms on the third and fourth lines can result from gauge invariant combinations such as
(\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{\dagger}\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}})^{2}
and
(\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}^{\dagger}{120}_{{\mathrm{H}}})^{2}
, respectively. Further, we assume that in Eqs. (
5.24
-
5.27
), all sextet fields are expressed in the mass basis. Eq. (
5.24
) identifies three distinct operators corresponding to
i,j=1,2
with
\eta_{12}=\eta_{21}
. Given that
M_{S_{1}}<M_{S_{2}}
, the most significant contribution to neutral baryon-antibaryon oscillations typically comes from the term corresponding to
\eta_{11}
in Eq. (
5.24
). Consequently, our analysis will focus primarily on the operators induced by
S_{1}
.

Figure 5.2:
Feynman graph depicting
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation, drawn using
[
Harlander:2020cyh
]

By integrating out various colour sextet fields from the vertices specified in Eqs. (
5.24
-
5.27
) and their corresponding hermitian conjugates, and using the diquark couplings computed in Eqs. (
5.7
), we derive an effective Lagrangian inducing baryon-antibaryon oscillations at the leading order, as shown below;

{\cal L}_{\rm eff}^{|\Delta B|=2}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\,c_{i}\,{\cal O}_{i}\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.},

(5.28)

with

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\varepsilon_{\sigma\rho\eta}\,\left(u^{C\dagger}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C*}_{\beta B}\right)\left(u^{C\dagger}_{\sigma C}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C*}_{\rho D}\right)\left(d^{C\dagger}_{\gamma E}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C*}_{\eta F}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\varepsilon_{\sigma\rho\eta}\,\left(d^{C\dagger}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C*}_{\sigma B}\right)\left(d^{C\dagger}_{\beta C}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C*}_{\rho D}\right)\left(u^{C\dagger}_{\gamma E}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,u^{C*}_{\eta F}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\varepsilon^{\sigma\rho\eta}\,\left(u^{\dagger}_{\alpha A}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{*}_{\beta B}\right)\left(u^{\dagger}_{\sigma C}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{*}_{\rho D}\right)\left(d^{CT}_{\gamma E}\,\,{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}\,\,d^{C}_{\eta F}\right)\,.

(5.29)

In the above Eq. (
5.4
), the quark fields are in their physical basis. The operators
{\cal O}_{1}
and
{\cal O}_{2}
are derived from the quartic terms found in Eq. (
5.24
) and Eq. (
5.25
), respectively. The subsequent terms, outlined in Eqs. (
5.26
, and
5.27
), both lead to a single operator, which is
{\cal O}_{3}
.

The effective strengths of the
n
-
\overline{n}
operators listed in Eq. (
5.4
) are integrating out the heavy sextet fields. We integrate out the sextet fields from Eq. (
5.10
) and substitute the expression in the vertices mentioned in Eqs. (
5.24
-
5.27
). This gives us the different six fermion operators mentioned in Eq. (
5.4
) along with its strength as mentioned below;

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\eta_{11}v_{\sigma}}{M_{\Sigma}^{2}M_{S_{1}}^{4}}\,\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{EF}\,\Big[\frac{4ic_{\theta}^{2}}{15\sqrt{15}}\,\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{AB}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{CD}\Big.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{8is_{\theta}^{2}}{3\sqrt{15}}\,\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{AB}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{CD}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Big.\frac{8\sqrt{2}ic_{\theta}s_{\theta}}{15\sqrt{3}}\,\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{AB}\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{CD}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4i}{15\sqrt{15}}\,\frac{\eta_{2}v_{\sigma}}{M_{\Sigma}^{4}M_{\cal S}^{2}}\,\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{AB}\,\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{CD}\,\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{u^{C}}^{*}\right)_{EF}\,

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v_{\sigma}}{M_{\Sigma}^{2}}\,\left(U_{d^{C}}^{T}FU_{d^{C}}\right)_{EF}\,\Big[\frac{24i\eta_{3}}{15\sqrt{15}M_{\mathbb{S}}^{4}}\,\left(U_{u}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d}^{*}\right)_{AB}\left(U_{u}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d}^{*}\right)_{CD}\Big.

(5.30)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Big.\frac{8i\eta_{4}}{3\sqrt{15}M_{\overline{S}}^{4}}\,\left(U_{u}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d}^{*}\right)_{AB}\left(U_{u}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d}^{*}\right)_{CD}\Big].\,

When
\sigma
acquires
vev
,
\langle\sigma\rangle\,\equiv\,v_{\sigma}
,
B-L
is violated by two units, as also discussed in subsection (
). The unitary matrices
U_{f}
and
U_{f^{C}}
, where
f=u,d
, are the rotation matrices within the flavour space and can be calculated directly from the corresponding quark mass matrices, as elaborated in the section (
). Further, the coefficients
c_{1}
and
c_{3}
receive contributions from both
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

We intend to map the above-mentioned operators, given in Eq. (
5.4
), with those listed in
[
Grojean:2018fus
,
Buchoff:2015qwa
]
and thus transform quark fields into left- and right-chiral notations using the relations specified in the Eq. (
1.3
). The resultant operators are shown below;

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{\sigma\rho\eta}\,\left(\overline{(u^{\alpha}_{RA})^{C}}\,d^{\beta}_{RB}\right)\,\left(\overline{(u^{\sigma}_{RC})^{C}}\,d^{\rho}_{RD}\right)\,\left(\overline{(d^{\gamma}_{RE})^{C}}\,d^{\eta}_{RF}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\varepsilon_{\sigma\rho\eta}\,\left(\overline{(d^{\alpha}_{RA})^{C}}\,d^{\sigma}_{RB}\right)\,\left(\overline{(d^{\beta}_{RC})^{C}}\,d^{\rho}_{RD}\right)\,\left(\overline{(u^{\gamma}_{RE})^{C}}\,u^{\eta}_{RF}\right)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{3}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,\epsilon_{\sigma\rho\eta}\,\left(\overline{(u^{\alpha}_{LA})^{C}}\,d^{\beta}_{LB}\right)\,\left(\overline{(u^{\sigma}_{LC})^{C}}\,d^{\rho}_{LD}\right)\,\left(\overline{(d^{\gamma}_{RE})^{C}}\,d^{\eta}_{RF}\right).\,

(5.31)

To compute
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation, we set
A=B=C=D=E=F=1
in the expression of
{\cal O}_{i}
and
c_{i}
given in Eqs. (
5.4
and
5.4
) respectively. Further, the operators
{\cal O}_{1}
and
{\cal O}^{*}_{3}
correspond to
{\cal O}_{1}
and
{\cal O}_{3}
of
[
Grojean:2018fus
,
Buchoff:2015qwa
]
, respectively. Additionally, the operator
{\cal{O}}_{2}
in our case, aligns with
{\cal O}^{1}_{RRR}
from
[
Buchoff:2015qwa
]
, which is itself a linear combination of the operators
{\cal O}_{1}
and
{\cal O}_{4}
, as shown below;

{\cal O}_{2}=\frac{1}{4}{\cal O}^{1}_{RRR}=\frac{1}{5}\left({\cal O}_{4}-12\,{\cal\tilde{O}}_{1}\right).\,

(5.32)

Notably,
{\cal O}_{4}
has a vanishing nuclear matrix element
[
Syritsyn:2016ijx
]
, whereas
{\cal\tilde{O}}_{1}
has a nuclear matrix element that is identical to that of
{\cal O}_{1}
. Consequently, in our analysis, the operator
{\cal O}_{2}
is directly linked to
{\cal O}_{1}
. This simplification results in only two linearly independent operators,
{\cal O}_{1}
and
{\cal O}_{3}
, written in Eq. (
5.4
) in our scenario.

The operators
{\cal O}_{1,3}
need to be evolved from the scale of the sextet masses, indicated by
\mu=M_{\Phi}
, down to
\mu_{0}=2
GeV.
\mu_{0}
typically represents the energy scale at which nuclear matrix elements are calculated using lattice techniques. However, the scale of
M_{\Phi}
is still ad-hoc as more than one scalars induce
n
-
\overline{n}
. Consequently, we define
M_{\Phi}
as the mean of the masses of the different sextets scalars involved in the
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation. Also, a key characteristic of the basis in which
{\cal O}_{1,3}
are expressed is that they are not going to mix with each other under RGE flow. The mean lifetime of the
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation can then be calculated using these effective operators as follows:

\displaystyle\tau_{n\bar{n}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1,3}\langle\bar{n}|{\cal O}_{i}(\mu_{0})|n\rangle\,c_{i}(\mu_{0})\right|\,,

(5.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1,3}\langle\bar{n}|{\cal O}_{i}(\mu_{0})|n\rangle\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}^{(4)}(m_{b})}{\alpha_{s}^{(4)}(\mu_{0})}\right)^{\frac{3\gamma^{(0)}_{i}}{50}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}^{(5)}(m_{t})}{\alpha_{s}^{(5)}(m_{b})}\right)^{\frac{3\gamma^{(0)}_{i}}{46}}\right.

\displaystyle\;\times\;\left.\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}^{(6)}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}^{(6)}(m_{t})}\right)^{\frac{\gamma^{(0)}_{i}}{14}}c_{i}(M_{\Phi})\right|,\,

where
\gamma^{(0)}_{i}
represents the leading order anomalous dimension of the operator
{\cal O}_{i}
, and
\alpha_{s}^{(n_{f})}
denotes the strong coupling constant for
n_{f}
flavours of light quarks to be computed at the top
(m_{t})
and bottom
(m_{b})
pole mass. We take
\gamma^{(0)}_{1}=4
and
\gamma^{(0)}_{3}=0
which are specified in
[
Buchoff:2015qwa
]
. Incorporating the results of the hadronic matrix elements of
O_{1}
and
O_{3}
computed from lattice computation, as given in
[
Syritsyn:2016ijx
]
and the running effects as described in
[
Grojean:2018fus
]
, we arrive at the following expression for oscillation time of neutron to the antineutron in our case:

\tau_{n\bar{n}}^{-1}=\left|0.760\,\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(M_{\Phi})}{\alpha_{s}(10^{5}\,{\rm GeV})}\right)^{2/7}\,\left(c_{1}-\frac{12}{5}\,c_{2}\right)\,+\,1.08\,c_{3}^{*}\right|\,\Lambda_{\rm QCD}^{6}\,.

(5.34)

The
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillation time in the given
SO(10)
model can be explicitly calculated by substituting
c_{1,3}
from Eq. (
5.4
) in the aforementioned relation given in Eq. (
5.34
).

Evidently,
(U_{u}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d}^{*})_{11}\neq 0
despite
G
being antisymmetric in the flavour space, as
U_{u}
and
U_{d}
are in general different. As a result, the colour sextet scalars residing in
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
can also contribute to non-vanishing
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillations together with the sextets residing in
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
.

5.5
Perturbativity of the Effective Quartic Couplings

From the expressions for
c_{i}
in Eq. (
5.4
), it is evident that maximising the
n
-
\overline{n}
transition rate would require a large
v_{\sigma}
and the presence of at least two of the three colour sextet fields at a lower scale. However, this scenario may lead to large negative effective quartic couplings for the light scalars
[
Babu:2002uu
]
. This issue stems from corrections induced by trilinear terms to the quartic coupling. These trilinear vertices are generated when
\sigma
acquires a
vev
, as shown in Eqs. (
5.24
-
5.27
). We focus on the first term, Eq. (
5.24
), to evaluate the constraints on the masses of these underlying scalars and calculate the correction to the quartic coupling of
\Sigma
,
i.e.

\lambda
, as shown in the diagram depicted in Fig. (
5.3
).

Figure 5.3:
Diagram showing quartic coupling of
\Sigma
and a correction due to trilinear coupling among three colour sextet scalars. The vertex marked by a bullet point represents a trilinear coupling generated by the VEV of the
B-L
charged scalar
\sigma
.

The correction to the tree-level quartic coupling from trilinear vertices is as follows:

-i\,\lambda_{\rm eff}=-i\,\lambda+i\,\delta\lambda,\,

(5.35)

where the computation of
\delta\lambda
for the quartic vertex appearing in Eq. (
5.24
) using the diagram shown in Fig. (
5.3
) in the limit of vanishing external momentum is given as follows:

\displaystyle i\,\delta\lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{i,j,k,l}\,\eta_{ij}\eta_{jk}^{*}\eta_{kl}\eta_{li}^{*}\,v_{\sigma}^{4}\,

(5.36)

\displaystyle\;\times\;\underbrace{\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{(p^{2}-M_{S_{i}}^{2})(p^{2}-M_{S_{j}}^{2})(p^{2}-M_{S_{k}}^{2})(p^{2}-M_{S_{l}}^{2})}}_{\equiv I_{ijkl}},\,

where the factor 4 in the above Eq. (
5.36
) is a symmetry factor. Further, computation of the diagram by putting
i,j,k,l=1,2
leads to the following;

\displaystyle i\,\frac{\delta\lambda}{4v_{\sigma}^{4}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\eta_{11}|^{4}\,I_{1111}+|\eta_{22}|^{4}\,I_{2222}+4|\eta_{12}|^{2}\left(|\eta_{11}|^{2}\,I_{1112}+|\eta_{22}|^{2}\,I_{2221}\right)\,

(5.37)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(2|\eta_{12}|^{4}+4\,{\rm Re}[\eta_{11}\eta_{22}\eta_{12}^{*}\eta_{12}^{*}]\right)\,I_{1122}.\,

It is evident from the diagram, Fig. (
5.3
) that the amplitude of the diagram is finite. Additionally, the different loop integrals appearing in the above Eq. (
5.37
) are defined as follows
[
Buras:2002vd
]
:

\displaystyle I_{iiii}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi^{2}}\,\frac{1}{24M_{S_{i}}^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle I_{iiij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi^{2}}\,\frac{M_{S_{i}}^{4}-M_{S_{j}}^{4}+2M_{S_{i}}^{2}M_{S_{j}}^{2}\,\log(M_{S_{j}}^{2}/M_{S_{i}}^{2})}{8M_{S_{i}}^{2}(M_{S_{i}}^{2}-M_{S_{j}}^{2})^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle I_{iijj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4\pi^{2}}\,\frac{1}{4(M_{S_{i}}^{2}-M_{S_{j}}^{2})^{2}}\,\left(\frac{M_{S_{i}}^{2}+M_{S_{j}}^{2}}{M_{S_{i}}^{2}-M_{S_{j}}^{2}}\,\log\left(\frac{M_{S_{i}}^{2}}{M_{S_{j}}^{2}}\right)-2\right)\,.

(5.38)

The integrals appearing in Eq. (
5.5
) modulo
i
yield positive results. The sole negative contribution to
\delta\lambda
could originate from the last term in Eq. (
5.37
). However, this term is insufficient to nullify the positive contributions from the other terms. As a result,
\delta\lambda
remains positive. For,
M_{S_{1}}\ll M_{S_{2}}
, Eq. (
5.37
) can be reduced to the following;

\lambda_{\rm eff}\simeq\lambda-\frac{|\eta_{11}|^{4}}{24\pi^{2}}\,\frac{v_{\sigma}^{4}}{M_{S_{1}}^{4}}\,.

(5.39)

If
\lambda_{\rm eff}<\lambda
,
SU(3)_{C}
vacuum would not be stable. Consequently, both
\lambda_{\rm eff}
and
\lambda
should be positive and perturbative, which leads to a constraint on the mass of sextet propagating inside the loop,
i.e.

M_{S_{1}}
.

M_{S_{1}}\geq\frac{|\eta_{11}|v_{\sigma}}{(24\pi^{2})^{1/4}}\,,

(5.40)

provided
M_{\Sigma}<v_{\sigma}
. If
M_{\Sigma}>v_{\sigma}
, the effective theory below
v_{\sigma}
is devoid of
\Sigma
and hence its quartic term. Consequently, the constraint mentioned in Eq. (
5.40
) is not applicable in such a scenario.

The above discussion on the perturbativity of quartic coupling can be easily extended to include the other sextet scalars and their interactions, as mentioned in Eqs. (
5.24
-
5.27
). Generally, when two or more sextet scalars are coupled through a
B-L
violating vertex, the
SU(3)_{C}
potential becomes unstable if all are significantly below the
B-L
breaking scale. To avoid this issue, at least one of these sextets must have a mass greater than the
B-L
breaking scale. Consequently, this requirement would increase
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation time in models with a high
B-L
breaking scale.

5.6
Baryogenesis

So far, we have considered the contribution of sextet scalar fields in generating
\Delta F=\Delta B=2
processes in the sections (
5.3
and
5.4
). Further, we now explore the potential of sextet scalars in generating the observed baryon asymmetry of the universe. The sextets scalar fields are also charged under
B-L
and, consequently, can also play a role in generating baryon asymmetry of the universe. One of the fundamental prerequisites to generate the baryon asymmetry is the presence of
B-L
violation, which is inherently in the
SO(10)
GUT framework. Furthermore, once we have
B-L
violation, it is immuned from its washout by electroweak sphalerons
[
Kuzmin:1987wn
,
Harvey:1990qw
]
.

The relevant sextet scalars for this include
\Sigma
,
S
,
\tilde{S}
(or
S_{1,2}
in the physical basis), as given in Eqs. (
5.10
and
5.24
), and the pertinent Lagrangian is shown as follows:

\displaystyle-{\cal L}

\displaystyle\supset

\displaystyle Y^{\Sigma}_{AB}\,d^{CT}_{\alpha A}\,C^{-1}\,d^{C}_{\beta B}\,\Sigma^{\alpha\beta}\,+\,Y^{S_{i}}_{AB}\,\epsilon^{\alpha\beta\gamma}\,u^{CT}_{\gamma A}\,C^{-1}\,d^{C}_{\sigma B}\,{S_{i}}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}\,

(5.41)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{ij}\,\sigma\,\Sigma^{\alpha\beta}\,{S_{i}}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\gamma}\,{S_{j}}^{\theta}_{\beta\theta}\,\;+\;\rm{H.c.}\,.

The Lagrangian described in the Eq. (
5.41
) contains all the essential conditions to generate baryon asymmetry, called Sakharov conditions
[
Sakharov:1967dj
]
. It naturally includes parity (P) violation since
\Sigma
interacts exclusively with right-chiral fermions, while CP violation can emerge from the phases in
\eta_{ij}
, which are elaborated upon below. Additionally, the
vev
of
\sigma
leads to
B-L
violation, as previously mentioned. The requirement for departure from thermal equilibrium is fulfilled through the out-of-equilibrium decays of
\Sigma
in an expanding universe, which will be further discussed below.

We assume the mass hierarchy
M_{\Sigma}\gg M_{S_{2}}>M_{S_{1}}\gg m_{t}
. The relevant scatterings and decays to compute baryogenesis can be read from Eq. (
5.41
) and are given as follows:

•

B
conserving: decay
\Sigma\to d^{C}_{A}\,d^{C}_{B}
and scatterings
\Sigma\,S_{i}^{*}\to d^{C}_{A}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{B}
,
\Sigma\,u^{C}\to S_{i}\,d^{C}
and
\Sigma\,\overline{d^{C}}_{A}\to S_{i}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{B}
,

•

B
violating: decay
\Sigma\to S_{i}^{*}\,S_{j}^{*}
and scatterings
S_{i}^{*}\,S_{j}^{*}\to d^{C}_{A}\,d^{C}_{B}
,
\Sigma\,S_{i}\to\overline{d^{C}}_{A}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{B}
,
S_{i}^{*}\,\overline{d^{C}}_{A}\to S_{j}\,d^{C}_{B}
,
\Sigma\,u^{C}_{A}\to S_{i}^{*}\,\overline{d^{C}}_{B}
and
\Sigma\,d^{C}_{A}\to S_{i}^{*}\,\overline{u^{C}}_{B}
,

together with their CP conjugate and inverse processes.

We begin by computing the CP asymmetry in the baryon number violating decay of
\Sigma\to S^{*}_{i}\,S^{*}_{j}
. As shown below, CP asymmetry can be generated from the interference between the tree and the one-loop process, which has an absorptive part;

\epsilon_{ij}=\frac{\Gamma[\Sigma\to S^{*}_{i}S^{*}_{j}]-\Gamma[\Sigma^{*}\to S_{i}S_{j}]}{\Gamma_{\rm tot}[\Sigma]}\,.

(5.42)

The relevant diagrams showing the tree and one loop level depicting the decay of
\Sigma
are shown in Fig. (
5.4
).

Figure 5.4:
The decays of the colour sextet scalar (
\Sigma
), both at tree level and through one-loop diagrams, contribute to the generation of CP asymmetry. The vertex marked by the bullet point represents a trilinear coupling, which is induced by the
vev
of the
B-L
charged scalar,
\sigma
, drawn using
[
Binosi:2008ig
]

The resulting leading order CP asymmetry is shown below;

\epsilon_{ij}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\,\frac{{\rm BR}[\Sigma\to S^{*}_{i}S^{*}_{j}]}{|\eta_{ij}|^{2}}\,\left(\sum_{k\neq i}\frac{x_{i/k}}{1-x_{i/k}}\,{\rm Im}\left[\eta^{*}_{ij}\eta_{jk}\,{\rm Tr}[Y^{S_{i}\dagger}Y^{S_{j}}]\right]\,+\,i\leftrightarrow j\right)\,,

(5.43)

with
x_{i/k}\equiv M_{S_{i}}^{2}/M_{S_{k}}^{2}
. Further,
\epsilon_{ij}=\epsilon_{ji}
as
\eta_{ij}=\eta_{ji}
in Eq. (
5.41
). The total CP asymmetry produced in decays of
\Sigma
will be sum over all the decays of
\Sigma
into different
S_{1,2}
:

\epsilon=\epsilon_{11}+\epsilon_{22}+2\epsilon_{12}\,,

(5.44)

The asymmetry created between the number densities of
S_{i}
and
S_{i}^{*}
, resulting from the out-of-equilibrium decay of
\Sigma
, is subsequently transferred to the SM right-chiral quarks through the decays of
S_{i}
. This transfer occurs at temperatures below the freeze-out point of the asymmetry, given that
M_{S_{i}}\ll M_{\Sigma}
and occurs when
S_{i}
exits thermal equilibrium.

The final baryon-to-entropy ratio is defined as;

Y_{B}=\frac{4}{3}\,\epsilon\,\frac{\kappa(K)}{g_{*}}.\,

(5.45)

where, the factor of
4/3
represents the total
B-L
charge generated after the decay of
\Sigma
. The parameter
g_{*}
denotes the effective number of relativistic degrees of freedom present at the time of decay, which is approximately 125, including
\Sigma
,
S_{1,2}
, and the SM particles. The function
\kappa(K)
is an efficiency factor that accounts for the potential washout of asymmetries due to inverse decays and various scattering processes previously discussed. The parameter
K
is crucial as it measures the out-of-equilibrium condition and is defined as follows:

K=\frac{\Gamma[\Sigma\to S_{i}^{*}\,S_{j}^{*}]}{2H(M_{\Sigma})},\,

(5.46)

together with the Hubble parameter as:

H(T)=1.66\,g_{*}^{1/2}\,\frac{T^{2}}{M_{P}}\,.

(5.47)

An exact value for
\kappa
is typically determined by numerically solving the full Boltzmann equations, as given in
[
Herrmann:2014fha
,
Fridell:2021gag
]
. However, an approximate analytical solution for
\kappa
is available and well-suited for the current setup. Assuming that
\Sigma
starts with an initial thermal abundance, the formula for
\kappa
is provided by
[
Buchmuller:2004nz
]
:

\kappa(x)=\frac{2}{x\,z_{B}(x)}\left(1-\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\,x\,z_{B}(x)\right]\right)\,,

(5.48)

such that,

z_{B}(x)=2+4\,x^{0.13}\,\exp\left[-\frac{2.5}{x}\right]\,.

(5.49)

The solution for
\kappa
mentioned above in Eq. (
5.48
) primarily accounts for washout effects from inverse decays and is applicable when
K\leq 10^{3}
. For values of
K
exceeding
10^{3}
, scattering processes become more significant, leading to an exponential decrease in
\kappa
. It is important to note that when
K\leq 1
,
\kappa(K)\to 1
indicates that there is no dilution in the baryon asymmetry due to the washout.

We aim to demonstrate the amount of CP violation, as described by Eq. (
5.44
), within our framework is sufficient to account for the observed baryon to entropy ratio of the universe,
i.e.

Y_{B}^{\rm exp}=(6.10\pm 0.04)\times 10^{-10}

[
Planck:2018vyg
]
. To achieve this, we first determine the maximum possible value of
\epsilon
and subsequently assess the amount of damping permitted through Eq. (
5.45
), ensuring that
Y_{B}\geq 6.0\times 10^{-10}
. Assuming the complex nature of
\eta_{ij}
, parameterising
\eta_{ij}\,=\,|\eta|\,\exp\left(\phi_{ij}\right)
and that the branching ratio
{\rm BR}[\Sigma\to S^{*}_{i}S^{*}_{j}]\approx 1/4
for all
i
and
j
in Eqs. (
5.43
and
5.44
), leads to the following maximum value of CP asymmetry;

\epsilon=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\frac{1+x_{1/2}}{1-x_{1/2}}\right)\,\left|{\rm Tr}[Y^{S_{1}\dagger}Y^{S_{2}}]\right|\,\left(\sin(\phi_{12}-\phi_{22}-\phi_{Y})-\sin(\phi_{12}-\phi_{11}+\phi_{Y})\right)\,,

(5.50)

where
\phi_{Y}\equiv{\mathrm{A}rg}({\rm Tr}[Y^{S_{1}\dagger}Y^{S_{2}}])
is the phase that arise from the Yukawa couplings. For
x_{1/2}\ll 1
, implying a large hierarchy between in masses of the decay product species, the maximum possible value of
\epsilon
becomes the following;

\epsilon_{\rm max}\simeq\frac{1}{\pi}\left|{\rm Tr}[Y^{S_{1}\dagger}Y^{S_{2}}]\right|\,.

(5.51)

Substituting
Y^{S_{1,2}}
from Eq. (
5.2
) and using
{\rm Tr}[F^{\dagger}G]=0
, which is due symmetricity and antisymmetricity of
F
and
G
respectively. We find the following maximum possible value of CP asymmetry in this case as follows;

\epsilon_{\rm max}\simeq\frac{2}{3\pi}\left|\sin 2\theta\,{\rm Tr}\left[G^{\dagger}G-\frac{1}{10}F^{\dagger}F\right]\right|\,.

(5.52)

Figure 5.5:
Values of
M_{\Sigma}
and
|\eta|v_{\sigma}
that are ruled out by
Y_{B}>6.0\times 10^{-10}
are indicated for two scenarios:
\epsilon_{\rm max}=10^{-7}
is represented by a lighter gray shade, and
\epsilon_{\rm max}=10^{-2}
by a darker gray.

Further, for
M_{S_{i}}\ll M_{\Sigma}
, one finds
\Gamma[\Sigma\to S_{i}^{*}\,S_{j}^{*}]\simeq\frac{|\eta_{ij}|^{2}v_{\sigma}^{2}}{16\pi M_{\Sigma}}
. Substituting this in the expression of
K
given in Eq. (
5.46
) and setting
|\eta_{ij}|=|\eta|
, we get

K=1.07\times\left(\frac{|\eta|v_{\sigma}}{10^{15}\,{\rm GeV}}\right)^{2}\times\left(\frac{10^{15}\,{\rm GeV}}{M_{\Sigma}}\right)^{3}\,.

(5.53)

Substituting
K
, shown in the above Eq. (
5.53
), into
\kappa(K)
given in Eq. (
5.48
), the baryon to entropy ratio
Y_{B}
can be calculated based on the given value of
\epsilon_{\rm max}
in Eq. (
5.52
). Adhering to the constraint that
Y_{B}\geq 6.0\times 10^{-10}
the allowed regions of
M_{\sigma}
as a function quartic coupling times the
vev
are shown in Fig. (
5.5
)
[
Patel:2022nek
]
.

We infer from Fig. (
5.5
) that to have a viable observed baryon asymmetry of the universe from the decay of
M_{\sigma}
, its mass cannot be less than the
B-L
scale.

The generation of baryon asymmetry discussed in this section relies on sextet fields from the Yukawa sectors of GUT scalars and requires both
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. In cases where only one particular irrep, either
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
or
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, is present in the Yukawa sector and gives rise to one sextet field, an additional sextet could emerge from
54_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. A similar mechanism to generate the asymmetry of the universe is still applicable
[
Babu:2012vc
]
. Further, scalars other than sextets can also be used to generate baryon assymetry
[
Hati:2018cqp
]
.

5.7
Constraints on the Sextets

We will now constraint the mass scales of various colour sextet scalars based on the several phenomenological implications discussed in previous sections, including quark flavour violation (section (
5.3
)),
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation (section (
5.4
)), constraints on the perturbativity of the quartic coupling (section (
5.5
)), and the ability to generate cosmological baryon asymmetry (section (
5.6
)). From the discussions of previous sections, it is apparent that the phenomena related to sextets depend on two critical parameters: (a) the Yukawa couplings with the quarks,
i.e.
the matrices
G
and
F
, and (b) the
B-L
breaking
v_{\sigma}
.
The Yukawa matrices of a realistic non-supersymmetric
SO(10)
model based on
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and the matrix
F
can be inferred from the Eq. (
3.2.2
) from the Chapter (
3
), while their explicit form can be inferred from the Appendix (
A
).

G\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}\\
-\lambda^{4}&0&\lambda^{2}\\
-\lambda^{3}&-\lambda^{2}&0\end{array}\right)\,,~~F\sim\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\alpha_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{5}&\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}\\
\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\end{array}\right).\,

We have omitted the
{\cal O}(1)
coefficients for each element of the matrices
F
and
G
. The matrix elements of
G
are assumed to be of similar magnitudes as of
F
, as observed in an earlier fit
[
Joshipura:2011nn
]
. The parameters
\alpha_{2,3}
, with
|\alpha_{2,3}|\lesssim 1
, measure the extent of Higgs doublet mixing as discussed in the section (
) of the previous Chapter (
3
).

Assuming that the light neutrino masses are generated via Type-I seesaw, consequently
v_{\sigma}
is constrained as follows,

v_{\sigma}=\alpha_{2}\,v^{\prime}_{S}\,\simeq\alpha_{2}\times 10^{15}\,{\rm GeV}\,,

(5.61)

where
v^{\prime}_{S}
is explicitly defined in
[
Mummidi:2021anm
]
and is found to be in the range
10^{14}
-
10^{15}
GeV. In addition to
v_{\sigma}
,
F
, and
G
, the analysis also requires unitary matrices,
U_{f}
and
U_{f^{C}}
, which diagonalise the quark mass matrices
M_{f}
for
f=u,d
. Their approximate forms can be referenced from the Eq. (
3.2.2
).

Further, we consider two example values for
\alpha_{2,3}
, which, in turn, constrains the
B-L
scale and order of entries appearing in
G
and
F
.

(i)

High-scale
B-L
(HS):
\alpha_{2,3}=\lambda
. This implies

v_{\sigma}\simeq 10^{14}\,{\rm GeV}\,,~~G\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\lambda^{7}&\lambda^{6}\\
-\lambda^{7}&0&\lambda^{5}\\
-\lambda^{6}&-\lambda^{5}&0\end{array}\right)\,,~~F\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{8}&\lambda^{7}&\lambda^{6}\\
\lambda^{7}&\lambda^{6}&\lambda^{5}\\
\lambda^{6}&\lambda^{5}&\lambda^{4}\end{array}\right)\,.

Given that
\alpha_{2,3}=\lambda
, the lightest pair of electroweak doublet Higgs notably includes significant contributions from the doublets residing in
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. Consequently, the entries of
G
and
F
must be relatively small in this context. Further,
\alpha_{2,3}
cannot be substantially greater than
\lambda
because, in such cases, the contribution of
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
to the fermion masses becomes negligible. And
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
individaully cannot reproduce the observed fermion mass spectrum
[
Joshipura:2011nn
]
.

(ii)

Intermediate-scale
B-L
(IS):
\alpha_{2,3}=\lambda^{5}
. This leads to the following.

v_{\sigma}\simeq 10^{11}\,{\rm GeV}\,,~~G\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}\\
-\lambda^{3}&0&\lambda\\
-\lambda^{2}&-\lambda&0\end{array}\right)\,,~~F\simeq\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\lambda^{4}&\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}\\
\lambda^{3}&\lambda^{2}&\lambda\\
\lambda^{2}&\lambda&1\end{array}\right)\,.

In this scenario,
G
and
F
exhibit relatively stronger couplings with the SM fermions, as the contributions from the doublets residing in
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
to the lightest Higgs are suppressed.

In a scenario where the
B-L
breaking scale,
i.e.
(
v_{\sigma}
), is significantly lower than
10^{11}
GeV,
\alpha_{2}
would need to be much less than
\lambda^{5}
. This condition might lead to non-perturbative values for some of the couplings in
F
within the class of realistic models. Additionally, a smaller
\alpha_{2}
with perturbative values for couplings in
F
suggests that the charged fermion masses predominantly originate from
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, a possibility which has been forbidden by the fits
[
Joshipura:2011nn
]
. Alternatively, the relationship between
v_{\sigma}
and the Yukawa couplings can be understood as follows:
M\,=\,v_{\sigma}F
is the right-handed neutrino Majorana mass matrix and the magnitude of the elements in
M_{R}
is dictated to reproduce the light neutrino masses via type I seesaw mechanism. Consequently, this necessitates smaller values for
F
when
v_{\sigma}
is close to the GUT scale and relatively larger values for
F
when
v_{\sigma}
assumes intermediate values.

Before estimating constraints on the sextet scalars for the HS and IS scenarios discussed in points
(i)
and
(ii)
, it is crucial to consider a model-independent limit on their masses derived from direct search experiments. Colour sextets can be pair-produced at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) via gluon fusion
[
Chen:2008hh
,
Han:2009ya
,
Richardson:2011df
]
. Notably, this production process is independent of the couplings with quarks, thus providing a stringent lower limit on the sextet’s masses.

The non-observation of deviations from SM predictions so far implies
[
Richardson:2011df
]
:

M_{\Phi}\geq 1\,{\rm TeV}\,.

(5.76)

Other direct search methods, such as resonant production and single top production, also put constraints on the sextet’s couplings with quarks. These methods are known to provide relatively milder limits when the couplings are small
[
Pascual-Dias:2020hxo
,
Fridell:2021gag
]
. As a result, we will use the robust lower limit obtained from gluon fusion, given in Eq. (
5.76
), and proceed to constrain the sextets within the mass range of
10^{3}
to
10^{16}
GeV. The latter bound on the mass of sextets comes from the effective theory approach.

5.7.1
Light
\Sigma
,
S_{1}

To begin with, we assume
|\eta_{11}|=1
and
\theta=\nicefrac{{\pi}}{{4}}
in Eq. (
5.4
). Further, together with the aforementioned assumption and using Yukawa couplings given in Eqs. (
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
3.2.2
), substituting it Eq. (
5.34
)), we compute the
n
-
\overline{n}
transition time as shown below. Further, as discussed towards the end of the section (
5.7
), we restrict the
M_{\Sigma}
as
1\,{\rm TeV}\leq M_{\Sigma},M_{S_{i}}<M_{\rm GUT}
while the remaining sextet fields stay close to the GUT scale
[
delAguila:1980qag
]
.

\displaystyle\left|\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{11}\right|\sim\left|\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{11}\right|\sim\left|\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{11}\right|\simeq\lambda^{8}\,,

\displaystyle\left|\left(U_{d^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{11}\right|\sim\left|\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}F^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{11}\right|\sim\left|\left(U_{u^{C}}^{\dagger}G^{*}U_{d^{C}}^{*}\right)_{11}\right|\simeq\lambda^{4}\,,

(5.77)

for both the HS and IS cases, respectively. Further, we calculate the contributions of

M_{\Phi}=(M_{\Sigma}+M_{S_{1}})/2
, as also discussed in the previous section. The derived limits on

and

,

Further,
S_{1}
and
\Sigma
cannot be simultaneously light as it will hamper the perturbativity of effectively generated quartic coupling, as discussed in the section (
5.5
) and is also displayed in the Fig. (
5.6
)
[

]
.

As shown in Fig. (
5.6
), in scenarios with a high-scale
v_{\sigma}
, either
\Sigma
or
S_{1}
could be significantly lighter than the GUT scale. However, the perturbativity of the quartic coupling restricts both from being simultaneously lighter than
10^{13}
GeV. Their weak couplings with quarks mean that a TeV-scale
\Sigma
or
S_{1}
could remain largely unconstrained by
|\Delta F|=2
or
|\Delta B|=2
processes. When
v_{\sigma}=10^{11}
GeV,
M_{S_{1}}
(or
M_{\Sigma}
) could be as low as 1 TeV (or 10 TeV) provided that the other sextet is heavier than
v_{\sigma}
. In this scenario, a lighter
M_{S_{1}}
leads to a comparatively faster neutron-antineutron transition time, as observed in the right panel of Fig. (
5.6
). Overall, constraints from the perturbativity of quartic couplings largely forbid the possibility of observing
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillations in the near-future experiments for both the high and intermediate
B-L
breaking scales.

Sub-GUT scale
\Sigma
and
S_{i}
might also explain the universe’s cosmological baryon asymmetry through thermal baryogenesis, as elaborated in the section (
5.6
). The maximum CP asymmetry calculated using Eq. (
5.52
) for the High and Intermediate
B-L
scale is reduced to be as follows:

\epsilon_{\rm max}=\begin{cases}{\cal O}(10^{-7}),&\text{for HS}\\
{\cal O}(10^{-2}),&\text{for IS}\\
\end{cases}

(5.78)

From the Fig. (
5.5
), we infer following constraint on the mass of
\Sigma

(M_{\Sigma})
.

M_{\Sigma}\gtrsim\begin{cases}2\times 10^{14}\,{\rm GeV,}&\text{for HS}\\
5\times 10^{10}\,{\rm GeV,}&\text{for IS}\\
\end{cases}

(5.79)

The region where the baryon asymmetry,
Y_{B}
, is at least
6.0\times 10^{-10}
, indicating it is favoured by baryogenesis generated by sextet fields, is also depicted in Fig. (
5.6
). For a very light
S_{1}
and
v_{\sigma}\approx 10^{11}
GeV, this region could potentially be investigated further through enhanced measurements of
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillations.

5.7.2
Light
\Sigma
,
{\cal S}

Assuming
|\eta_{2}|=1
in Eq. (
5.25
), we evaluate constraints on a light
\Sigma
and
{\cal S}
while keeping other sextets at the GUT scale. Unlike
S_{1,2}
,
{\cal S}
only interacts with up-type quarks and induces
D^{0}
-
\overline{D}^{0}
oscillations at the tree level as well as the loop level, which significantly restricts strongly coupled TeV-scale
{\cal S}
.

Figure 5.7:

and

for the high (left panel) and intermediate (right panel)
B-L
breaking scale. The additional details are the same as those in the caption of Fig. (

).

It is evident from Fig. (
5.7
) that limits from meson-antimeson oscillations and the perturbativity of the effective quartic coupling suggest that an observable
n
-
\bar{n}
transition rate in near-future experiments is unlikely in realistic renormalisable
SO(10)
models.

5.7.3
Light
\Sigma
,
\mathbb{S}

Along similar lines, we examine a scenario where light
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{S}
are considered while the remaining sextets have their masses at the GUT scale. The findings are presented in Fig. (
5.8
). For this analysis, we set
|\eta_{3}|=1
as earlier cases and explore two scenarios regarding the values of
F
and
v_{\sigma}
as previously discussed. Unlike
S_{i}
, the electroweak triplet
\mathbb{S}
mediates quark flavour violating interactions at tree level in both the up- and down-type quark sectors. This leads to a relatively high upper bound on
M_{\mathbb{S}}
when larger Yukawa couplings are involved. The constraints arising from
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillations and the perturbativity of the effective quartic coupling are comparable to those seen with light
\Sigma
and
S_{1}
. Alone, sub-GUT scale
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{S}
are insufficient for generating baryon asymmetry, and an additional
\Sigma
-like scalar would be necessary for viable baryogenesis, as discussed in the section (
5.6
).

and
\mathbb{S}
for the high (left panel) and intermediate (right panel)
B-L
breaking scale. The additional details are the same as those in the caption of Fig. (

).

5.7.4
Light
\Sigma
,
\overline{S}

At last,

Figure 5.9:

and

.

we explore the scenario where
1\,{\rm TeV}\leq M_{\Sigma},M_{\overline{S}}<M_{\rm GUT}
, while remaining sextets are at the GUT scale. Setting
|\eta_{4}|=1
, the results after performing the similar as the previous cases are displayed in Fig. (
5.9
). Moreover,
\overline{S}
resides in
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and interacts with left-chiral up- and down-type quarks through flavour anti-symmetric couplings. As shown in
Y^{\overline{S}}
in Eq. (
5.2
), its diagonal couplings disappear when
U_{u}=U_{d}
; however, this condition does not appear in the usual cases. Consequently, effective diagonal couplings are generated by quark mixing, leading to a non-zero rate for
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillations at the leading order. Similar to
S_{1,2}
,
\overline{S}
also mediates the
|\Delta F|=2
processes at the 1-loop level. Nonetheless, these processes impose more stringent constraints on the light
\overline{S}
compared to
S_{1,2}
, as illustrated in Figs. (
5.6
, and
5.9
). This stricter limit is due to
\overline{S}
’s relatively large Yukawa coupling due to different Clebsch-Gordan factors. Overall, the constraints on the masses of sub-GUT scale
\mathbb{S}
and
\overline{S}
appear similar.

5.8
Summary

S
CALARS TRANSFORMING UNDER the two-index symmetric representations of the
SU(3)_{C}
of the SM gauge group have been studied for their diverse phenomenological implications in the context of bottom-up approaches in this chapter. We assess the potential for these sextets to emerge from the realistic renormalisable
SO(10)
which naturally accommodates five distinct colour sextet fields shown in Tab. (
5.1
). We derive their interactions with quarks and calculate effective operators that contribute to electrically neutral meson-antimeson in the section (
5.3
) and baryon-antibaryon oscillations in the section (
5.4
). The latter is influenced by
B-L
breaking, induced by the
vev
of an SM singlet field
\sigma
. Additionally, we analyse the effective quartic coupling of the sextet scalars, which is prone to the perturbativity constraints from
B-L
breaking effects, as discussed in section (
5.5
). Further, we propose a rather simple way of generating the baryon asymmetry with these sextets in the section (
5.6
). The key findings of this chapter are summarised below:

•

Four pairs of sextets—
\Sigma
-
S
,
\Sigma
-
\mathcal{S}
,
\Sigma
-
\mathbb{S}
, and
\Sigma
-
\overline{S}
— can induce neutron-antineutron observable oscillation in near-future experiments if both sextets in a pair are as light as
10^{5}
to
10^{8}
GeV. However, this scenario is largely ruled out by the need for perturbative quartic couplings in models with
v_{\sigma}\geq 10^{11}
GeV.

•

Observable
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillations along with perturbative effective quartic couplings could occur if
v_{\sigma}<10^{8}
GeV and the sextets’ couplings with quarks are around
{\cal O}(1)
. Yet, this typically results in too light right-handed neutrinos, making type I seesaw mechanism non-viable in realistic
SO(10)
models.

•

If
M_{\Sigma}>10^{11}
GeV,
S
could be as light as on the order of TeV, while
\mathbb{S}
,
\overline{S}
, and
{\cal S}
might be
\gtrsim 10
TeV. Conversely, for
S
,
\mathbb{S}
,
\overline{S}
, and
{\cal S}
heavier than
10^{11}
GeV,
M_{\Sigma}
could be around
10
TeV or more. Such constraints for these lighter sextets arise mainly from meson-antimeson oscillations and/or direct searches.

•

SO(10)
models featuring both
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
in the Yukawa sector exhibits a pair of sextets,
S_{1,2}
, having the same quantum numbers as
S
. With
\Sigma
heavier than
v_{\sigma}
and
S_{1,2}\ll M_{\Sigma}
, this scenario offers a promising way to generate the cosmological universe’s baryon asymmetry.

Many insights from this study are based on the strong correlation between the couplings of sextets with quarks and the
B-L
scale within renormalisable
SO(10)
GUTs. These parameters cannot vary freely as often presumed in bottom-up approaches.

Further, it is important to note that baryon asymmetry can also be produced through thermal leptogenesis, given that lepton number violation and right-handed neutrinos are integral components of
SO(10)
GUTs
[
Mummidi:2021anm
]
. In scenarios where thermal leptogenesis fails to account for the observed asymmetry—due to insufficient CP violation in the lepton sector or an inappropriate mass spectrum and couplings of the right-handed neutrinos—the sextet-driven cosmological baryogenesis outlined in this Chapter offers a feasible alternative.

In Chapters (
3
) and (
4
), we constrained the scalar fields capable of destabilising a nucleon. Chapter (
5
) explored the various phenomena a sextet field can contribute to. Some proton decay mediators could also contribute to flavour violation,
n
-
\bar{n}
oscillations, and potentially generate baryogenesis, but the proton decay constraints provide stringent mass limits. Broadly speaking, in Chapters (
3
) and (
4
), we investigated the phenomenology of all the scalar fields capable of contributing to the renormalisable and non-renormalisable Yukawa sector of
SO(10)
and constrained them in a top-down approach (see Tabs. (
2.2
and
2.3
)). In the renormalisable sector, the scalar fields we have not yet studied include
t(1,3,2)
and
O(8,2,\frac{1}{2})
along with their conjugate partners (cf. Tab.
2.2
). The
t
field is known to contribute to the Type-II seesaw mechanism
[
Melfo:2010gf
]
and can contribute to lepton-flavour-violating processes
[
Calibbi:2009wk
]
. Additionally, the
O
field does not carry any
B-L
charge and can induce quark-flavour-violating processes
[
Manohar:2006ga
]
. We anticipate that the constraints on the
SU(3)
octets (
i.e.
,
O
) will be similar to those on the sextets coming from flavour violation, albeit
{\cal O}(1-10)
suppressed or enhanced due to different Clebsch-Gordan coefficients.

The investigations performed in Chapters (
3
,
4
and
5
) show how a well-defined model at higher energy levels can constrain new physics possibilities at lower energies, rendering the model testable and potentially falsifiable.

Chapter 6
Scalar Induced Corrections to the Yukawa Correlations

This page is intentionally left blank.

Chapter 7
Conclusions and Outlook

I
N THIS THESIS, we have explored various facets of scalar fields and their direct and indirect implications in the framework of GUTs, demonstrating new particles, in particular the scalar fields, can guide and dictate the future direction of hidden physics, alias the New Physics.

GUTs, which aim to unify the known fundamental interactions, also place quarks and leptons within the same multiplet. This unification necessitates the presence of scalar irreps, whose dimensionality is sometimes larger than that of the fermion and (occasionally) gauge boson irreps. These scalar irreps, comprised of many scalars including SM Higgs, are essential for elementary requirements to break the bigger gauge symmetry into SM gauge symmetry, able to reproduce observed fermion masses and mixing angles. Each scalar field is associated with a physical energy scale and imparts significant implications. These implications can be direct and indirect, manifesting at lower energies.

In Chapter (
2
), Tabs. (
2.2
and
2.3
) list the scalars that have been focused on in this thesis. Reviewing these tables will also summarise the Chapters (
3
,
4
, and,
5
). We begin with Tab. (
2.2
), which lists the scalars residing in the
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
,
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
dimensional irreps. Many of these scalars are charged under
B-L
, meaning they will induce processes that affect the Baryon and Lepton numbers. There are only four distinct
B-L
charges that different scalars can have, and the relationship between
B-L
,
U(1)_{X}
, and
U(1)_{Y}
is dictated by Eq. (
2.3
). These four distinct
B-L
charges are :
0
,
\pm\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}
,
\pm\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}
and
\pm 2
. Below, we consider each
B-L
charge individually and explain the associated phenomenology of the scalars with that specific
B-L
charge, which has also been summarised in Tabs. (
7.1
and
7.2
).

The scalars
D
and
O
, together with their conjugate partners, exhibit
B-L=0
. The scalar
D
is present in all the irreps contributing to the renormalisable Yukawa sector and can be responsible for Electroweak symmetry breaking and the Dirac mass of charged and neutral fermions. The scalars
O
and
\overline{O}
couple solely to quarks and can be constrained by quark flavour violation.

The scalars
T
,
\Theta
,
{\cal T}
, and
{\mathbb{T}}
along with their conjugates have
B-L\,=\,\big|\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}\big|
. The scalars
T
,
\Theta
,
{\cal T}
, and
{\mathbb{T}}
exhibits diquark and leptoquark couplings, which can be obtained through the methodology outlined in Chapter (
2
). These scalars can affect the stability of the proton and induce proton decay through mass dimension six
(D=6)
operators. Our analysis reveals that only
T
and
\mathbb{T}
can induce proton decays at tree level, and
{\cal T}
can induce proton decays at loop level. The induced proton decays by these scalars are
B-L
conserving. Further,
\Theta
only exhibits diquark coupling whose Yukawa couplings are antisymmetric and can induce
B-L
violating proton decays. In a realistic
SO(10)
model based on
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, only
T
and
\overline{T}
can induce
B-L
conserving proton decays and proton favours to decay into the second generation mesons as elaborated in Chapter (
3
). As these scalars induce proton decay, they cannot remain light in a realistic
SO(10)
scenario and the minimum mass
T
can acquire adhering to proton decay constraint is
10^{11}
GeV.

The non-renormalisable interactions in GUTs, which offer an alternate solution to the inconsistencies of renormalisable GUTs in UV, can also affect proton stability. In non-renormalisable
SO(10)
models, the smallest irrep
16_{\rm{H}}
couples with the
\mathbf{16}
-plet, contributing to the fermion masses at the non-renormalisable level.
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
exhibit pairs of scalars that also have
B-L\,=\,\big|\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}\big|
and exhibits diquark and leptoquark vertices, which are shown in Tab. (
7.2
). In Chapter (
4
), we studied the implications of such pairs of scalars in inducing
D=6
proton decays. It was argued that
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
can mimic the role of
126_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, and the proton decay spectrum was computed in a realistic model based on the same. The pattern of branching of the proton is similar to the findings of Chapter (
3
). Unlike renormalisable
SO(10)
GUTs, where a single scalar mediates decay at leading order, non-renormalisable models involve pairs of scalars, which can potentially remain closer to the electroweak scale than the GUT scale.

The scalars
S
,
\Sigma
,
{\cal S}
, and
{\mathbb{S}}
, which also have
B-L\,=\,\big|\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}\big|
, are termed as sextet scalars. These sextets solely couple to diquarks and hence cannot induce leading order proton decay and have been extensively studied in Chapter (
5
). They induce flavour violations and baryon number-violating phenomena like neutral baryon-antibaryon oscillation. The
n
-
\overline{n}
oscillation is induced by three sextets and requires a source of baryon number violation in the theory. Further,
S
is the only the only sextet which is degenerate and is present both in
120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. This speciality of
S
is harnessed in Chapter (
5
) to study the cosmological implications of sextet scalars in generating the observed baryon-asymmetry of the universe.

Scalars with
B-L=\big|\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}\big|
exhibit only leptoquark couplings and can be a source of loop-induced lepton and quark flavour violations. These scalars are
\Delta
and
\Omega
, along with their conjugate partners. However, they can interact with scalars having
B-L=\big|\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}\big|
, which exhibit diquark couplings and induce nucleon decays. These nucleon decays differ from the
D=6
,
B-L
conserving nucleon decays, occurring instead at
D=7
and violating
B-L
by two units. Such nucleon decays in the framework of realistic GUTs have been thoroughly examined in Chapter (
3
) and is summarised in Tab. (
7.1
).

Scalars with
B-L=\big|2\big|
, such as
\sigma
,
s
,
\overline{s}
,
X
, and
t
, exhibit only dilepton couplings. The
vev
of
\sigma
violates
B-L
by two units, generates the Majorana mass of RH neutrinos, and cannot attain values less than
10^{11}
GeV in a minimal realistic
SO(10)
models as the Yukawa couplings would become non-perturbative, discussed in Chapter (
5
). The
vev
of
\sigma
also contributes to
B-L
violating processes such as proton decay and neutron-antineutron oscillation, as demonstrated in Chapters (
3
and
5
). Further,
t
is known to yield light neutrino masses via the Type-II seesaw mechanism.
In Chapters (
3
,
4
, and
5
), studied direct implications induced by the scalars have been summarised in Tabs. (
7.1
and
7.2
).

One of the immediate consequences of the mass bounds summarised in Tabs. (
7.1
and
7.2
) can be studied in the context of gauge coupling unification. As can be inferred from Fig. (
1.1
), the SM gauge couplings do not precisely unify in this case. The masses of the scalars affect the running of the gauge couplings, and the exact expressions can be found in
[
Cheng:1973nv
]
. For simplicity, we consider a one-loop analysis for gauge coupling unification. The running depends on the one-loop coefficients
b_{i}
, which vary for different scalars. To account for the effects of scalar masses,
b_{i}
is replaced by
B_{i}=b_{i}+\sum_{I}b_{iI}\,\frac{\ln\left(\nicefrac{{M_{\rm{GUT}}}}{{M_{I}}}\right)}{\ln\left(\nicefrac{{M_{\rm{GUT}}}}{{M_{Z}}}\right)}
, where
M_{\rm{GUT}}
,
M_{I}
, and
M_{Z}
represent the GUT scale, the mass of the scalar, and the Z-boson mass scale, respectively
[
Giveon:1991zm
]
. For successful unification at one loop, the following conditions are to be achieved
[
Dorsner:2005fq
]
:

\displaystyle{}\frac{B_{23}}{B_{12}}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle 0.719,

\displaystyle\ln\left(\frac{M_{\rm{GUT}}}{M_{\rm{Z}}}\right)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{184.9}{B_{12}},

(7.32)

where
B_{23}=B_{2}-B_{3}
and
B_{12}=B_{1}-B_{2}
. The values of
B_{12}
and
B_{23}
for various scalars appearing in Tab. (
7.1
) can be inferred from
[
Patel:2011eh
]
. The SM value of the ratio
\frac{B_{23}}{B_{12}}=0.53
implies that for successful unification,
B_{23}
must increase and
B_{12}
should decrease. Scalars such as
\Delta
,
\overline{\Delta}
,
\mathbb{S}
,
\overline{\mathbb{S}}
,
t
,
\mathbb{T}
, and
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
has this property and contribute to gauge coupling unification if they remain light. These scalars can also impart threshold corrections at an intermediate scale. The study of precision gauge coupling unification requires two-loop analysis and one-loop threshold correction which is beyond the scope of this thesis.

In Chapter (
6
), we explored the indirect implication of scalars in affecting low-energy observables like Yukawa couplings. The observationally inconsistent relationship
Y_{d}=Y_{e}^{T}
in non-supersymmetric
SU(5)
GUTs can be corrected at the one-loop level by switching on quantum corrections induced by heavy scalars in an otherwise minimally extended
SU(5)
framework by singlet fermion(s). A mass hierarchy of at least two orders is needed between triplet and singlet(s) to yield ample threshold corrections. These quantum corrections can significantly alter conclusions about the minimal Yukawa sector, motivating further investigation in supersymmetric and non-supersymmetric variants of
SU(5)
and
SO(10)
GUTs.

Table 7.2:
Pair of scalars residing in
16_{{\mathrm{H}}}
capable of mediating tree and loop level proton decays. The last column shows the bound on the scalar, which does not acquire
vev
from the tree-level proton decays, where
\langle\hat{H}\rangle\,\sim\,246
GeV has been used. ”—” represents the respective pair of scalars is unconstrained from proton decay.

This thesis presents a systematic, careful, and comprehensive analysis of the scalar sector of grand unified models in its ability to directly influence the new physics within the context of concrete minimal and realistic
SO(10)
models and their variations. The implications of scalars, together with the stringent bounds, are computed in Chapters (
3
,
4
, and
5
).These stringent bounds are indirect and are due to various phenomenological implications. Another way to compute the bounds on the scalars is to evaluate one loop structure of the
SO(10)
scalar potential
[
Bertolini:2009es
,
Bertolini:2012im
,
Bertolini:2013vta
,
Held:2022hnw
,
Jarkovska:2023zwv
]
, and obtain the bounds in terms of several parameters entering in the scalar potential. These studies have also revealed the impacts of quantum corrections in enabling breaking chains that are not allowed at the tree level. Through our computed bounds, one can constrain the parameters of the scalar potential, leading to a more precise understanding of the scalar potential of realistic
SO(10)
models.

The obtained bounds on the scalars are also important as scalars are associated with a physical energy scale that can affect gauge coupling unification
[
Dorsner:2012pp
]
and influence the running of baryon number and lepton number violating operators, including their flavour dependence. Such precision computations are necessary for constraining and detecting new physics.

As exemplified in Chapter (
6
), the scalars play a significant role in constructing a minimal
SU(5)
model, and the discussed
SU(5)
model employs the Type-I seesaw mechanism to yield light neutrino masses. Another variant of this framework would be to implement the Type-II seesaw mechanism and to investigate whether loop-corrected Yukawa relations in a minimal
SU(5)
model with
5_{\mathrm{H}}
and
15_{\mathrm{H}}
contributing to the Yukawa sector can yield a realistic fermion mass spectrum and whether the seesaw scale can be lowered compared to the Type-I scenario. Furthermore, a yet another variant would be to expand the fermion sector of the minimal
SU(5)
model with a
\mathbf{24}
-fermion multiplet and to examine whether loop-corrected Yukawa relations, together with neutrino masses due to the Type-III seesaw mechanism, can yield the observed fermion mass spectrum. It would be interesting to check the possibility of achieving gauge coupling unification in both of the above-mentioned scenarios of the minimal
SU(5)
model with either the Type-II or III seesaw mechanism or both.

The other extension of the loop-corrected Yukawa relations would be considering the variants of
SO(10)
model. As mentioned, a single
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
-dimensional Higgs in the Yukawa sector yields generational mass degeneracy across all charged and neutral fermions. An interesting question arises about whether this issue could be rectified by introducing two or more copies of the
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
-dimensional irrep. An
SO(10)
model with
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}+120_{{\mathrm{H}}}
is also known to yield inconsistent tree-level mass relations. It would be a significant improvement if quantum corrections can render this model viable. Further, evaluating one-loop level Yukawa relations in an
SO(10)
model based on
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}+\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
-dimensional Higgs fields would be highly interesting and valuable for precision calculations. Furthermore, the supersymmetric extensions of the GUT model will be another case to consider in which there are known solutions to the doublet-triplet splitting problem.

S
CALARS SHALL PLAY a pivotal role in constructing a calculable, optimal/minimal, realistic, and renormalisable GUT model, testable through gravitational wave observations, baryon number-violating processes like nucleon decay and baryon-antibaryon oscillation, and can also explain observational cosmological evidence. The scalar sector of any GUT model is vast, and it offers a Pandora’s box of many interesting and challenging outcomes.
