Appendix A
Algebra of
SO(N)
and
SU(N)

The formal definition of a mathematical object called a
group
is as follows. Let
G
be a set (finite or infinite) and
o
be a binary operation.
\left(G,o\right)
is called a group if it satisfies the following axioms;

1.

Closure.

\forall

g_{1,2}\,\epsilon\,G
,
g_{1}\,o\,g_{2}\,\epsilon\,G
.

2.

Existence of Identity
.

\exists

e\,\epsilon\,G
such that
e\,o\,g\,=\,g\,o\,e\,=\,e
,
\forall\,g\,\epsilon\,G
.

3.

Existence of Inverse
.
\forall\,g\,\epsilon\,G

\exists\,g^{-1}\,\epsilon\,G
, such that
g\,o\,g^{-1}\,=\,g^{-1}\,o\,g\,=\,e
.

4.

Associative
.
\forall\,g_{1,2,3}\,\epsilon\,G,

\left(g_{1}\,o\,g_{2}\right)\,o\,g_{3}\,=\,g_{1}\,\left(g_{2}\,o\,g_{3}\right)
.

Below, we review some properties of
SO(N)
and
SU(N)
groups.

A.1
SO(N)
in nutshell

This section overviews the algebra associated with the
O(N)
group
[
Langacker:1980js
,
Slansky:1981yr
,
Wilczek:1981iz
,
Georgi:2000vve
,
Zee:2016fuk
,
Pal_2019
]
).
O(N)
denotes a group of orthogonal transformations in an
N
-dimensional space. These transformations preserve the norm (length) of the vector during rotations within an
N
-dimensional space. Let
R
be the
N\times N
matrix defined in an
N
-dimensional space with real elements. Then, the definition of orthogonal transformation is as follows;

\displaystyle{}R^{T}\,R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathds{1},

(A.1)

where,
R^{T}_{ij}\,\equiv\,R_{ji}
and
{\mathds{1}}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix. We call the orthogonal transformation a special-orthogonal transformation when the determinant of
R
is
1
. Set of all
N\times N
matrices satisfying Eq. (
A.1
) with the additional constraint of
\left|R\right|\,=\,1
forms a group under the binary operation of matrix multiplication and is called
SO(N)
. Of course, a set of matrices with only
\left|R\right|\,=\,-1
do not constitute a group as it lacks an identity element. Further, it is straightforward to compute the number of independent components in an orthogonal matrix, as shown below;

\displaystyle{}\#_{\text{Independent elements}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N\big(N-1\big)}{2}.

(A.2)

The number of independent components equals the total number of planes in an
N
-dimensional space where a rotation could be carried out. A
N
-dimensional vector transforms as follows;

\displaystyle{}X_{i}\to X^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{ij}\,X_{j},

(A.3)

where,
i
is the i
th
-component of the N-dimensional vector in
SO(10)
.
R_{ij}
is an element of
SO(N)
, which is a Lie group and thus can be parameterised as a perturbation near an identity element, as shown below:

\displaystyle{}R_{ij}\left(\omega\right)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}+\omega_{ij}\,M_{ij},

(A.4)

where,
M_{ij}
are the linearly independent generators of the group with
\omega_{ij}
s being the infinitesimal transformation parameters. In a sense,
M_{ij}
represents the direction normal to the plane formed by the axis
i-j
through which a rotation occurs, and the magnitude of rotation is determined by
\omega
. Substituting Eq. (
A.4
) into Eq. (
A.1
) constricts the
M_{ij}
s to obey the following commutation relation, sometimes also referred to as the
algebra
of the group:

\displaystyle{}\left[M_{ij},M_{kl}\right]\;\equiv\;M_{ij}\,M_{kl}-M_{kl}\,M_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left(\delta_{ik}\,M_{jl}-\delta_{il}\,M_{jk}-\delta_{jk}\,M_{il}+\delta_{jl}\,M_{ik}\right)

It is evident that when the indices
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
, the commutator in Eq. (
A.1
) vanishes identically, meaning
\left[M_{12},M_{13}\right]\,=\,0
and so forth. The number of generators that mutually commute is said to be the
rank
of the algebra. For both
SO(2N)
and
\,SO(2N+1)
, this rank remains the same and equals
N
. The matrix representation of
M_{ij}
satisfying the algebra mentioned in Eq. (
A.1
) is as follows;

\displaystyle{}\left(M_{ij}\right)_{mn}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\,\left(\,\delta_{im}\,\delta_{jn}-\delta_{in}\,\delta_{jm}\,\right).

(A.6)

The above relation represents the
mn
-th element of the generator
M_{ij}
, with
i\,\neq\,j
. The general element
R
of the group
SO(N)
can be parameterised in terms of
\omega
and
M
as follows;

\displaystyle{}R\left(\omega\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2}\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{i,j=N}\omega_{ij}\,M_{ij}\right),

(A.7)

where,
\omega
and
M
are antisymmetric in
ij
and the factor
\frac{1}{2}
is due to the over counting. The expression above Eq. (
A.7
) shows the transformation of a vector representation. One can also build similar transformation rules for tensors built from vector representations.

Further, let
r_{i}
be the elements of a vector defined in an
2N
-dimensional space, where
1\leq\,i\,\leq\,2N
. Under orthogonal transformation the quadratic form
\sum_{i=1}^{i=2N}\,r_{i}^{2}
remains invariant. Moreover, we can demand the following invariance:

\displaystyle{}\sum_{i=1}^{i=2N}\,r_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{i=2N}\,\left(\gamma_{i}\,r_{i}\right)^{2},

(A.8)

where
\gamma_{i}
are the
2N
Hermitian matrices. The above condition given in Eq. (
A.8
) is only satisfied if the
\gamma
matrices fulfil the following constraint:

\displaystyle{}\left\{\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}\right\}\;\equiv\;\gamma_{i}\,\gamma_{j}+\gamma_{j}\,\gamma_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\delta_{ij},

(A.9)

The algebra given in Eq. (
A.9
) is called
Clifford Algebra
and says that different
\gamma
matrices anticommute with each other. An example of matrices satisfying the Clifford algebra is the Pauli matrices. With the introduction of the Pauli matrices (
\sigma_{1,2,3}
), the correspondence between
SU(2)
and
SO(3)
becomes evident as follows;

\displaystyle{}x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{1}\,x\,+\,\sigma_{2}\,y\,+\,\sigma_{3}\,z\right)^{2}.

(A.10)

The immediate consequence of the relation, given in Eq. (
A.9
), on
\gamma_{i}
s are: i)
Tr\big(\gamma_{i}\big)\,=\,1\big)
, ii)
\gamma_{i}^{2}\,=\,1
. Since the
\gamma
matrices are traceless and their square is the identity, it implies that their eigenvalues must be
\pm 1
. Consequently,
\gamma
matrices are only defined in even dimensions.

The transformation given in Eq. (
A.3
) induces a transformation in
\gamma
matrices as follows;

\displaystyle{}\gamma_{i}\;\to\;\gamma^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{ij}\,\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\left\{\gamma^{\prime}_{i},\gamma^{\prime}_{j}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{ik}\,\left\{\gamma_{k},\gamma_{l}\right\}\,R_{lj}\;=\;2\,\delta_{ij}.

(A.11)

Since the transformed
\gamma^{\prime}
matrices also satisfy the same Clifford algebra as the untransformed ones, they must be related to the original
\gamma
matrices via a similarity transformation as follows;

\displaystyle{}\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}\big(R\big)^{-1}\,\gamma\,{\mathcal{T}}\big(R\big).

(A.12)

Thus for every transformation
R
in
2N-
dimensional space, we can associate
{\mathcal{T}}\big(R\big)
in
2^{N}
-dimensional space which acts on an
2^{N}
dimensional object and is referred to as a
spinor representation
. The transformed object in that
2^{N}
dimensional space is called a
spinor
. The transformation rule of a spinor is as follows:

\displaystyle{}\varphi_{i}\;\to\;\varphi^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}\big(R\big)_{ij}\,\varphi_{j}

(A.13)

Analogous to Eq. (
A.4
), the
{\cal{T}}\big(R\big)
can be expressed as follows.

\displaystyle{}{\cal{T}}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}\,+\frac{i}{2}\,\Omega_{ij}\,N_{ij},\,

(A.14)

Again,
N_{ij}
generates the rotation in
i-j
plane and the amount of rotation is
\Omega_{ij}
. A solution of
N_{ij}
satisfying the Eq. (
A.12
) is as follows;

\displaystyle{}N_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4}\,\left[\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}\right]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow{\cal{T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{i}{4}\,\Omega_{ij}\,N_{ij}\right).

(A.15)

{\cal{T}}
is the
2^{N}\times 2^{N}
-dimensional matrix through which a
2^{N}
-dimensional spinor transforms. As it is evident from the transformation rules of vector representation given in Eq. (
A.7
) and spinor transformation rule given in Eq. (
A.1
), under a
2\pi
rotation the vector representation remains invariant, while the spinor representation picks up a negative sign. Moreover, a spinor representation is invariant under a
4\pi
rotation.

Along the similar lines, we can define
\gamma_{F}\,\equiv\,\big(-i\big)^{N}\,\prod_{i=1}^{i=2N}\,\gamma_{i}
, and
P_{\pm}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\,\left(1\pm\gamma_{F}\right)
.
P_{\pm}
are called the projection operators with the properties; i)
P_{\pm}^{2}\,=\,P_{\pm}
, ii)
P_{+}\,P_{-}\,=\,P_{-}\,P_{+}\,=\,0
, and iii)
P_{+}\,+\,P_{-}\,=\,1
. Additionally,
\gamma_{F}
anticommutes with
\gamma_{i}
and commutes with
N_{ij}
. The action of
P_{\pm}
on
\varphi^{\prime}
yields
P_{\pm}\varphi^{\prime}=P_{\pm}\exp\left(-\frac{i}{4}\Omega_{ij}N_{ij}\right)\varphi
. Since
P_{\pm}
commutes with
N_{ij}
,
P_{\pm}\varphi^{\prime}=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{4}\Omega_{ij}N_{ij}\right)P_{\pm}\varphi
, thus, the projected spinor transforms similarly to the original spinor. Consequently, one spinor representation of
SO(2N)
decomposes into two irreducible spinor representations of dimension
2^{N-1}
. The connection between
P_{+}\varphi\equiv\varphi_{+}
and
P_{-}\varphi\,\equiv\,\varphi_{-}
is established via charge conjugation operator (
{\cal{C}}
). The exact nature of
{\cal{C}}
depends upon the number of dimensions (
N
) and its relation with the spinorial generators is as follows
[
Zee:2016fuk
]
:

\displaystyle{}{\cal{C}}^{-1}\,\left(N_{ij}\,\left(1\pm\gamma_{\rm{F}}\right)\,\right)^{*}\,{\cal{C}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-N_{ij}\,\left(1\pm\big(-1\big)^{N}\,\gamma_{F}\right).

(A.16)

The expression in Eq. (
A.16
) specifies that for
SO(8m)
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\varphi_{\pm}^{*}=\varphi_{\pm}
; for
SO(8m+4)
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\varphi_{\pm}^{*}=-\varphi_{\pm}
; and for
SO(4m+2)
, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\varphi_{\pm}^{*}=\varphi_{\mp}
. In the case of
SO(8m)
, the irreps are termed real; for
SO(8m+4)
, they are termed pseudo-real; and for
SO(4m+2)
, they are complex conjugates of each other.

The direct product of two spinorial irreps of
SO(4m+2)
can be expressed in terms of the antisymmetric product of
\gamma
’s mentioned in Eq. (
A.9
). For
SO(2N)
, there are
2N

\gamma
matrices
[
Zee:2016fuk
]
;

\displaystyle{}\varphi_{\pm}\times\varphi_{\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[1]\oplus[3]\oplus[5]...\oplus[2N-1],

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pm}\times\varphi_{\mp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[0]\oplus[2]\oplus[4]...\oplus[2N],

(A.17)

where,
[i]
represent antisymmetric product of
i
number of
\gamma
matrices.
SO(2N)
comprises various subgroups derived from orthogonal groups and unitary groups. The decomposition of irreps of
SO(2M+2N)\to SO(2M)\times SO(2N)
is as follows
[
Zee:2016fuk
]
;

\displaystyle{}2_{+}^{M+N-1}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\left(2_{+}^{M-1},2_{+}^{N-1}\right)\oplus\left(2_{-}^{M-1},2_{-}^{N-1}\right),

\displaystyle 2_{-}^{M+N-1}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\left(2_{+}^{M-1},2_{-}^{N-1}\right)\oplus\left(2_{-}^{M-1},2_{+}^{N-1}\right),

(A.18)

where,
2_{\pm}^{M-1}
and
2_{\pm}^{N-1}
are the irreps of
SO(2M)
and
SO(2N)
respectively. Similarly,
U(N)
can be naturally embedded in
SO(2N)
and the decomposition of the fundamental irrep of
SO(2N)
into the fundamental irrep of
U(N)
is as follows:

\displaystyle{}2N

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle N\oplus\bar{N}.

(A.19)

So far, we have discussed the characteristics of the
SO(2N)
group. Now, we will focus on a specific case, the
SO(10)
group, and outline its properties, which are enumerated below:

(i)

SO(10)
is a rank five group and exhibits complex spinorial irreps.

(ii)

The fundamental irrep in
SO(10)
is of
10
- dimensional. It also exhibits a
32
-dimensional spinorial representation, which can be decomposed into two sixteen-dimensional irreps
16_{\pm}
.

(iii)

The tensor multiplication of two-
16
-plet of
SO(10)
is as follows:

\displaystyle{}16_{\pm}\times 16_{\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 10\oplus 120\oplus\overline{126},

\displaystyle 16_{\pm}\times 16_{\mp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\oplus 45\oplus 210,

(A.20)

where
10
,
120
, and
126
denote the number of independent degrees of freedom in one, three, and five-indexed antisymmetric tensors, respectively. Similarly,
45
,
210
, and
1
represent the number of independent degrees of freedom in two, four, and entirely antisymmetric tensors, respectively.

We now conclude this section and discuss the properties of
U(N)
group in the next section.

A.2
U(N)
in nutshell

Unitary transformations are the sets of transformations that preserve the norm of a vector in a complex vector space. Let
U
be an
N\times N
matrix with complex elements; the definition of a unitary transformation is as follows:

\displaystyle{}U^{\dagger}\,U

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathds{1}},

(A.21)

where,
U^{\dagger}_{ij}\equiv U^{*}_{ji}
and
{\mathds{1}}
in
N\times N
identity matrix. Likewise, similar to orthogonal matrices, unitary matrices also have a determinant of
\pm 1
, that is,
\left|U\right|=\pm 1
. All sets of
N\times N
matrices satisfying Eq. (
A.21
) form a group under the binary operation of matrix multiplication and are termed as the unitary group
U(N)
. Furthermore, the set of all matrices with determinant 1, i.e.,
\left|U\right|=1
, is known to form the special unitary group
(SU(N))
under matrix multiplication. The number of independent components in a unitary matrix in
N^{2}
and the number of independent components in
SU(N)
matrix is, thus,
N^{2}-1
.

A general
SU(N)
transformation can be parameterised as follows:

\displaystyle{}U

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-i\,\sum_{i=1}^{i=N^{2}-1}\theta_{i}\,T_{i}\right),

(A.22)

where,
\theta
are real parameters determining the amount of transformation and
T_{a}
are Hermitian and traceless generators. The fundamental representation
\varrho
of
SU(N)
transforms accordingly with the following relation;

\displaystyle{}\varrho^{i}\,\to\,\varrho^{i^{\prime}}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,U^{i}_{j}\,\varrho^{j}.

(A.23)

Similarly, the conjugate representation, denoted as
\bar{N}
, is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{}\varrho_{i}\,\equiv\,\big(\varrho^{i}\big)^{*}\,\to\,\varrho_{i}^{\prime}\,\equiv\,U_{i}^{j}\,\varrho_{j},\hskip 28.45274ptU_{i}^{j}\,=\,\big(U^{i}_{j}\big)^{*}.

(A.24)

The tensor product of two
N
-dimensional fundamental irrep of
SU(N)
transforms into two indexed antisymmetric and symmetric tensors as shown below;

\displaystyle{}\varrho^{i}\times\varrho^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{\left[ij\right]}\oplus\Phi^{\{ij\}},

\displaystyle N\times_{A,S}N

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N(N-1)}{2}\oplus\frac{N(N+1)}{2}.

(A.25)

The tensor
\Phi^{[ij]}
is antisymmetric in
ij
with
\frac{N(N-1)}{2}
independent degrees of freedom, while
\Phi^{\{ij\}}
is symmetric in
ij
with
\frac{N(N+1)}{2}
independent degrees of freedom. Moreover, the symbol
\times_{A}

(\times_{S})
denotes the antisymmetric (symmetric) tensor product.

The tensor product of fundamental and conjugate representation is as follows;

\displaystyle{}\varrho^{i}\times\varrho_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{i}_{j}\oplus\delta^{i}_{j}\,\phi,

\displaystyle N\times\overline{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N^{2}-1\oplus 1.

(A.26)

The tensor
\Phi^{i}_{j}
is traceless with
N^{2}-1
independent degrees of freedom, while
\phi
is a singlet and has only one degree of freedom.

The decomposition of
SU(N)
into its subgroups is as follows
[
Zee:2016fuk
]
:

\displaystyle{}SU(M+N)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle SU(M)\times SU(N)\times U(1)

\displaystyle M+N

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\left(M,1,\frac{1}{M}\right)\oplus\left(1,N,-\frac{1}{N}\right),

\displaystyle(M+N)\times\overline{\big(M+N\big)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\big(M+N\big)^{2}-1\right)\oplus 1

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\big(M^{2}-1,1,0\big)\oplus\big(1,N^{2}-1,0\big),

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle\,\big(M,\bar{N},\frac{1}{M}-\frac{1}{N}\big)\oplus\big(\bar{M},N,\frac{1}{N}-\frac{1}{M}\big)\oplus\big(1,1,0\big),

\displaystyle\big(M+N\big)\times_{A,S}\big(M+N\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(M_{A,S},1,\frac{1}{M}\big)\oplus\big(1,N_{A,S},\frac{1}{N}\big)\oplus\big(M,N,-\frac{1}{M}-\frac{1}{N}\big),

where,
M_{S}

\big(M_{A}\big)
are the symmetric (antisymmetric) components of
M
.

This page is intentionally left blank.

Appendix B
Decay Width Expressions &
\chi^{2}
Definition

B.1
Proton Decay Width Relations

The proton decay width expressions computed from chiral perturbation theory are as follows
[
Nath:2006ut
,
Beneito:2023xbk
]
;

\displaystyle\Gamma[p\to e_{i}^{+}\pi^{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m_{\pi^{0}}^{2})^{2}}{32\,\pi\,m_{p}^{3}f_{\pi}^{2}}A^{2}\times\left(\frac{1+\tilde{D}+\tilde{F}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\Big(\left|\alpha\,y[u_{1},d_{1},e^{C}_{i},u^{C}_{1}]+\beta\,y^{\prime*}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{1},e^{C}_{i},u^{C}_{1}]\right|^{2}\Big.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big.\left|\alpha\,y^{*}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{1},e_{i},u_{1}]+\beta\,y^{\prime}[u_{1},d_{1},e_{i},u_{1}]\right|^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle\Gamma[p\to\overline{\nu}\pi^{+}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m_{\pi^{\pm}}^{2})^{2}}{32\,\pi\,m_{p}^{3}f_{\pi}^{2}}A^{2}\left(1+\tilde{D}+\tilde{F}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{i=1}^{3}\left|\alpha\,y^{*}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{1},\nu_{i},d_{1}]+\beta\,y^{\prime}[u_{1},d_{1},\nu_{i},d_{1}]\right|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma[p\to e_{i}^{+}K^{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m_{K^{0}}^{2})^{2}}{32\,\pi\,m_{p}^{3}f_{\pi}^{2}}A^{2}\,\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Big|C_{Li}^{-}-C_{Ri}^{-}+\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}(\tilde{D}-\tilde{F})\left(C_{Li}^{+}-C_{Ri}^{+}\right)\Big|^{2}\Big.

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big.\Big|C_{Li}^{-}+C_{Ri}^{-}+\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}(\tilde{D}-\tilde{F})\left(C_{Li}^{+}+C_{Ri}^{+}\right)\Big|^{2}\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma[p\to\overline{\nu}K^{+}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m_{K^{\pm}}^{2})^{2}}{32\,\pi\,m_{p}^{3}f_{\pi}^{2}}\,A^{2}\,\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Big|\frac{2\tilde{D}}{3}\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}C^{\nu}_{L1i}+\left(1+\frac{\tilde{D}+3\tilde{F}}{3}\frac{m_{p}}{m_{B}}\right)C^{\nu}_{L2i}\Big|^{2},

\displaystyle\Gamma[p\to e_{i}^{+}\eta]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(m_{p}^{2}-m_{\eta}^{2})^{2}}{32\,\pi\,m_{p}^{3}f_{\pi}^{2}}\,A^{2}\,\frac{1}{6}\Big[\Big|C_{Li}^{+}(1-\tilde{D}+3\tilde{F})-2C_{Li}^{-}\Big|^{2}\,

(B.1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\Big.\Big|C_{Ri}^{+}(1-\tilde{D}+3\tilde{F})-2C_{Ri}^{-}\Big|^{2}\Big]\,\Big.,

where,

\displaystyle C^{\pm}_{Li}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\,y^{*}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{2},e_{i},u_{1}]\pm\beta\,y^{\prime}[u_{1},d_{2},e_{i},u_{1}]\,,

\displaystyle C^{\pm}_{Ri}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\,y[u_{1},d_{2},e^{C}_{i},u^{C}_{1}]\pm\beta\,y^{\prime*}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{2},e^{C}_{i},u^{C}_{1}]\,,

\displaystyle C^{\nu}_{L1i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\,y^{*}[u_{1}^{C},d_{2}^{C},\nu_{i},d_{1}]+\beta\,y^{\prime}[u_{1},d_{2},\nu_{i},d_{1}]\,,

\displaystyle C^{\nu}_{L2i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\,y^{*}[u_{1}^{C},d_{1}^{C},\nu_{i},d_{2}]+\beta\,y^{\prime}[u_{1},d_{1},\nu_{i},d_{2}]\,.

(B.2)

Here,
m_{H}
denotes the mass of hadron
H
(
H=p,\pi^{0},\pi^{\pm},K^{0},K^{\pm},\eta
),
m_{B}
is average baryon mass and
f_{\pi}
is pion decay constant.
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\tilde{D}
and
\tilde{F}
are the parameters of the chiral Lagrangian. The factor
A
accounts for the renormalisation effects in hadronic matrix elements from the weak scale to the
m_{p}
.

B.2
Coefficients for Computing Proton Decays

The definition of
C
given in Eq. (
B.1
) is slightly modified to compute the branching pattern for proton decays in non-renormalisable
SO(10)
model and is shown as follows;

\displaystyle C^{\pm}_{Li}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\,c_{3}^{*}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{2},u_{1},e_{i}]\pm\beta\,c_{1}[u_{1},d_{2},u_{1},e_{i}]\,,

\displaystyle C^{\pm}_{Ri}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha\,c_{2}^{*}[u_{1},d_{2},u^{C}_{1},e^{C}_{i}]\pm\beta\,c_{4}[u^{C}_{1},d^{C}_{2},u^{C}_{1},e^{C}_{i}]\,,

\displaystyle C^{\nu}_{L1i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha\,c_{3}^{*}[u_{1}^{C},d_{2}^{C},d_{1},\nu_{i}]-\beta\,c_{1}[u_{1},d_{2},d_{1},\nu_{i}]\,,

\displaystyle C^{\nu}_{L2i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha\,c_{3}^{*}[u_{1}^{C},d_{1}^{C},d_{2}]-\beta\,c_{1}[u_{1},d_{1},d_{2},\nu_{i}]\,.

(B.3)

B.3
Solution for
{10}_{{\mathrm{H}}}+\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
model

This section explains elementary aspects of fermion mass fittings in a class of renormalisable
SO(10)
model with
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
participating in the Yukawa sector. As pointed out in chapter (
3
), the model requires complex
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
instead of real
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
. The complexification of
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
introduces extra couplings in the Yukawa sector (
H
and
\tilde{H}
). The extra Yukawa coupling
(\tilde{H})
can be forbidden by imposing a Peccei-Quinn symmetry under which
\mathbf{16}\,\to\,\exp(i\alpha)\,\mathbf{16}
and
\big(10_{{\mathrm{H}}},\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\big)\,\to\,\exp(-i2\alpha)\,\big(10_{{\mathrm{H}}},\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}\big)
.

The Yukawa coupling of SM-like Higgs doublet with SM fermions can be inferred from Eqs. (
2.4.2
,
,
). As the doublets,
D
and
\overline{D}
, resides both in
10_{{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}
, one can define
h_{u}
and
h_{d}
as their lightest linear combination as follows:

\displaystyle{}D_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{1}\,h_{d}\;\;\text{and}\;\;D_{\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\;=\;\beta_{2}\,h_{u}

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{10_{{\mathrm{H}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\,h_{d}\;\;\text{and}\;\;\overline{D}_{\overline{126}_{{\mathrm{H}}}}\;=\;\alpha_{2}\,h_{u}

(B.4)

where,
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
is subjected to follow
|\alpha_{1}^{2}|+|\alpha_{2}^{2}|\,\leq\,1\,\geq\,|\beta_{1}^{2}|+|\beta_{2}^{2}|

The effective Yukawa relations can be written using the Eqs. (
2.4.2
,
,
) as follows:

\displaystyle Y_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{\prime}+F^{\prime}\,,~~Y_{u}=r\,(H^{\prime}+sF^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle Y_{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{\prime}-3F^{\prime}\,,~~Y_{\nu}=r\,(H^{\prime}-3sF^{\prime})\,,~~M_{R}=v_{S}^{\prime}\,F^{\prime}\,,

(B.5)

where,

\displaystyle H^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\,\alpha_{1}\,2\sqrt{2}\,H,\;\;F^{\prime}\,=\,i\,\alpha_{2}\,2\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,F,\;\;r\,=\,\frac{\beta_{1}}{\alpha_{1}},\;\;s\,=\,\frac{\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}}{\alpha_{2}\beta_{1}},\;\;v^{\prime}_{s}\,=\,\frac{v_{s}}{\alpha_{2}}

v_{s}
is the
vev
of the singlet field
\sigma
(cf. Tab. (
2.2
)). The light neutrino mass matrix is obtained as
M_{\nu}=-v_{u}^{2}\,Y_{\nu}\,M_{R}^{-1}\,Y_{\nu}^{T}
, where
v_{u,d}=\langle h_{u,d}\rangle
,
v_{u}^{2}+v_{d}^{2}\equiv v^{2}=(174\,{\rm GeV})^{2}
and
v_{u}/v_{d}\equiv\tan\beta=1.5
. The mass matrices for the charged fermions are given by
M_{d,e}\equiv v_{d}\,Y_{d,e}
and
M_{u}\equiv v_{u}Y_{u}
.

The viability of the model in its capacity to yield observed charged and neutral fermion masses and mixing angles is done using the
\chi^{2}
optimisation method whose definition is as follows:

\displaystyle{}\chi^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\Bigg(\frac{O^{\rm{th}}_{i}\,-\,O^{\rm{exp}}_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}\Bigg)^{2}

(B.7)

The index
i
runs over a total of 19 observables, including nine charged lepton masses, four quark mixing parameters, two neutral fermion mass-squared differences, and three mixing angles and one Dirac phase (or two Majorana phases) in the neutrino sector. The theoretically computed value of the
i
-th observable is denoted by
O_{i}^{\text{th}}
, while
O_{i}^{\text{exp}}
represents the corresponding experimental value at the GUT scale. The experimental values of these 19 observables at the GUT scale can be found in
[
Mummidi:2021anm
]
. The uncertainty associated with the
i
-th observable,
\sigma_{i}
, is taken as
30\%
for the light quarks (
y_{u}
,
y_{d}
, and
y_{s}
) and
10\%
for the other observables. The fits performed in
[
Mummidi:2021anm
]
also account for the baryon-to-photon ratio but only consider three mixing angles in the neutrino sector.

Below, the necessary parameters of the best-fit solution obtained in the recent work
[
Mummidi:2021anm
]
are given. One finds the best-fit parameters for the Yukawa couplings as;

\displaystyle H^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0.00023&0&0\\
0&-0.04811&0\\
0&0&-5.79504\\
\end{array}\right)\times 10^{-3}\,,

\displaystyle F^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-0.0088+0.0178i&0.0475\,-0.0889i&0.4635\,+0.6797i\\
0.0475\,-0.0889i&1.1279\,+0.5108i&-1.2218-2.5921i\\
0.4635\,+0.6797i&-1.2218-2.5921i&5.4683\,-5.9856i\\
\end{array}\right)\times 10^{-4}\,.

Also,
r=77.4189
,
s=0.3140-0.0282\,i
and
v_{S}^{\prime}=9.84\times 10^{14}\,{\rm GeV}
